To do so, we will use the results of the previous section by considering a finite number of points
(X_{n})_{n\geq 0}
from the continuous interpolation.

For some
N>0
, we will first equip
\mathcal{X}^{N}
with the distance

(C.3.1)

\dist_{N}(\xi,\zeta)=\max_{0\leq n\leq N-1}\lVert\xi_{n}-\zeta_{n}\rVert

and bound the difference between the discrete rescaled trajectory and the continuous interpolation.

Lemma C.4
.

Fix
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{X}
compact,
\eta_{0}>0
,
N\geq 1
.
Then, there exists some constant
c=c(\mathcal{K},\eta_{0},N,f,\mathsf{U},\Omega)<+\infty
such that, for any
x_{0}\in\mathcal{K}
,
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0}]
,

(C.3.2)

\dist_{N}(x^{\eta},(X_{n})_{0\leq n\leq N-1})=\max_{0\leq n\leq N-1}\lVert x^{\eta}_{n}-X_{n}\rVert\leq c\eta\,.

Proof.

By
Lemma

B.2
, there is some compact set
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
that depends on
\mathcal{K}
,
N
,
\eta_{0}
and
M
such that, for any
x_{0}\in\mathcal{K}
,
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0}]
,
n\leq\lceil 1/\eta\rceil N
,
x_{n}
belongs to
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
.
In particular, for almost every
t\in[0,N-1]
, we have, that,

(C.3.3)

\displaystyle\dot{X}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{x_{\lfloor t/\eta\rfloor+1}-x_{\lfloor t/\eta\rfloor}}{\eta}=-\nabla f(x_{\lfloor t/\eta\rfloor})+\mathsf{U}(x_{\lfloor t/\eta\rfloor},\omega_{\lfloor t/\eta\rfloor})

with
x_{\lfloor t/\eta\rfloor}
belonging to
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
since
\left\lfloor\frac{t}{\eta}\right\rfloor\leq\left\lfloor\frac{N-1}{\eta}\right\rfloor\leq\left\lceil\frac{N}{\eta}\right\rceil
.
Hence, the norm of
\dot{X}_{t}
is bounded by some constant
B
for almost every
t\in[0,N-1]
uniformly in
x_{0}\in\mathcal{K}
,
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0}]
by the growth condition in
Item

(b.5pt)
.

Therefore, for any
0\leq n\leq N-1
, since
x^{\eta}_{n}=x_{n\lfloor 1/\eta\rfloor}=X_{n\eta\lfloor 1/\eta\rfloor}
, we have that

(C.3.4)

\lVert x^{\eta}_{n}-X_{n}\rVert\leq Bn\left\lvert 1-\eta\lfloor 1/\eta\rfloor\right\rvert\leq BN\eta

which concludes the proof.
∎

Now, for
N\geq 0
,
\xi=(\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{N-1})\in\mathcal{X}^{N}
, let us define the normalized discrete action functional

(C.3.5)

\mathcal{A}_{N}(\xi)\coloneqq\sum_{n=0}^{N-2}\rho(\xi_{n},\xi_{n+1})

where the cost of moving from one iteration to the next is defined for any
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
from the previous continuous normalized action functional (cf.
Eq.

C.2.1
) with horizon
1
as

(C.3.6)

\rho(x,x^{\prime})\coloneqq\inf\{\mathcal{S}_{0,1}(\gamma):\gamma\in\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathcal{X}),\gamma_{0}=x,\gamma_{1}=x^{\prime}\}\,.

Again, we show that the set of discrete trajectories with low action functional

(C.3.7)

\Gamma_{N}^{\mathcal{K}}(s)\coloneqq\{\xi\in\mathcal{X}^{N}:\xi_{0}\in\mathcal{K},\mathcal{A}_{N}(\xi)\leq s\}

is compact and
\mathcal{A}_{N}
is
lsc
.

Lemma C.5
.

Fix
N\geq 0
.
For any
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{X}
compact,
s\geq 0
, the set
\Gamma_{N}^{\mathcal{K}}(s)
is compact and
\mathcal{A}_{N}
is
lsc
on
\mathcal{X}^{N}
.

Proof.

First, let us show that, for
s\geq 0
,
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{X}
, the set
\{(x,x^{\prime})\in\mathcal{K}\times\mathcal{X}:\rho(x,x^{\prime})\leq s\}
is compact.
Let
(x^{k},x^{\prime k})_{k\geq 0}
be a sequence in
\mathcal{K}\times\mathcal{X}
such that
\rho(x^{k},x^{\prime k})\leq s
for all
k\geq 0
.
Then, for any
k\geq 0
, there exists
\gamma^{k}\in\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathcal{X})
such that
\gamma^{k}_{0}=x^{k}
,
\gamma^{k}_{1}=x^{\prime k}
and
\mathcal{S}_{0,1}(\gamma^{k})\leq\rho(x^{k},x^{\prime k})+(1+k)^{-1}
.
In particular, for any
k\geq 0
,
\gamma^{k}
belongs to
\{\gamma\in\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathcal{X}):\gamma_{0}\in\mathcal{K},\mathcal{S}_{0,1}(\gamma)\leq s+1\}
which is compact by
Lemma

C.3
.
Hence, there exists a subsequence that converges uniformly to some
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathcal{X})
.
Without loss of generality, assume that
\gamma^{k}\to\gamma
as
k\to\infty
.
In particular,
(x^{k},x^{\prime k})
converges to
(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})
as
k\to\infty
with
\gamma_{0}
belonging to
\mathcal{K}
.
Then, by
lsc
of
\mathcal{S}_{0,1}
, we have that
\mathcal{S}_{0,1}(\gamma)\leq\lim\inf_{k\to\infty}\rho(x^{k},x^{\prime k})\leq s
so that
\rho(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\leq s
.
Therefore, we have shown that
\{(x,x^{\prime})\in\mathcal{K}\times\mathcal{X}:\rho(x,x^{\prime})\leq s\}
is compact.

As a consequence
\rho
is
lsc
on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
: indeed, for any
s\geq 0
, any convergent sequence of points of
\{(x,x^{\prime})\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}:\rho(x,x^{\prime})\leq s\}
must be included in
\{(x,x^{\prime})\in\mathcal{K}\times\mathcal{X}:\rho(x,x^{\prime})\leq s\}
for some
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{X}
compact, which is closed and therefore contains the limit point of any such sequences.

\mathcal{A}_{N}
is then immediately
lsc
on
\mathcal{X}^{N}
.

Finally, the compactness of
\Gamma_{N}^{\mathcal{K}}(s)
follows by induction on
N
.
For
N=1
,
\Gamma_{1}^{\mathcal{K}}(s)=\{(x,x^{\prime})\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}:\rho(x,x^{\prime})\leq s\}
which is compact by the previous argument for any compact set
\mathcal{K}
.
Now, assume that
\Gamma_{N}^{\mathcal{K}}(s)
is compact for some
N\geq 1
.
As a consequence, the set

(C.3.8)

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\coloneqq\{x\in\mathcal{X}:\exists(\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{N-2})\in\mathcal{X}^{N-1}\,,(\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{N-2},x)\in\Gamma_{N}^{\mathcal{K}}(s)\}

is compact as well.
Hence,
\Gamma_{N+1}^{\mathcal{K}}(s)
is included in teh product of compact sets
\Gamma_{N-1}^{\mathcal{K}}(s)\times\Gamma_{1}^{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}(s)
and is therefore bounded.
Moreover,
\Gamma_{N+1}^{\mathcal{K}}(s)
is closed by
lsc
of
\mathcal{A}_{N}
and therefore compact.
This concludes the proof by induction and the proof of the lemma.
∎

We will first provide a discrete analogue of
Proposition

C.1
for the interpolated trajectory at times
n=0,\dots,N-1
, with
\eta^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{N}
the action functional in
\mathcal{X}^{N}
of the process
(X_{n})_{0\leq n\leq N-1}
, uniformly with respect to the starting point
x_{0}
in any compact set
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{X}
, as
\eta\to 0
.

Proposition C.2
.

Fix
N\geq 0
.
For any
s,\delta,\varepsilon>0
,
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{X}
compact, there exists
\eta_{0}>0
such that, for any
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0}]
, for any
x_{0}\in\mathcal{K}
,
\xi\in\Gamma_{N}^{\{x_{0}\}}(s)
, we have that

(C.3.9a)

\displaystyle\prob_{x_{0}}\left(\dist_{N}((X_{n})_{0\leq n\leq N-1},\xi)<\delta\right)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(-\frac{\mathcal{A}_{N}(\xi)+\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)

and

(C.3.9b)

\displaystyle\prob_{\!#1}\left(\dist_{N}((X_{n})_{0\leq n\leq N-1},\Gamma_{N}^{\{x_{0}\}}(s))>\delta\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{s-\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,.

for all
\xi\in\Gamma_{N}^{\{x_{0}\}}(s)

Proof.

Invoke the first part of
Proposition

C.1
with
T\leftarrow N-1
and
s\leftarrow s+\varepsilon
.
There exists
\eta_{0}>0
such that, for any
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0}]
, for any
x_{0}\in\mathcal{K}
,
\gamma\in\Gamma_{0,N}^{\{x_{0}\}}(s+\varepsilon)
,

(C.3.10)

\prob_{x_{0}}\left(\dist_{0,N-1}(X,\gamma)<\delta\right)\geq\exp\left(-\frac{\mathcal{S}_{0,N-1}(\gamma)+\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,.

Take
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0}]
,
x_{0}\in\mathcal{K}
,
\xi\in\Gamma_{N}^{\{x_{0}\}}(s)
.
Then, there exists
\gamma\in\Gamma_{0,N}^{x_{0}}(s+\varepsilon)
, for any
0\leq n\leq N-1
,
\gamma_{n}=\xi_{n}
, cf. (
C.3.6
).
Hence,

\displaystyle\prob_{x_{0}}\left(\dist_{N}((X_{n})_{0\leq n\leq N-1},\xi)<\delta\right)

\displaystyle\geq\prob_{x_{0}}\left(\dist_{0,N-1}(X,\gamma)<\delta\right)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(-\frac{\mathcal{S}_{0,N-1}(\gamma)+\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)

(C.3.11)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(-\frac{\mathcal{A}_{N}(\xi)+2\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)

which prove the first part of the result.

For the second part, we have similarly from
Proposition

C.1
with
T\leftarrow N-1
and
\delta\leftarrow\delta/2
that for any
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0}]
, for any
x_{0}\in\mathcal{K}
,

(C.3.12)

\prob_{x_{0}}\left(\dist_{0,N-1}(X,\Gamma_{0,N-1}^{\{x_{0}\}}(s))>\delta/2\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{s-\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,.

Now, note that if
\dist_{0,N-1}(X,\Gamma_{0,N-1}^{\{x_{0}\}}(s))<\delta
, then there must exist
\gamma\in\Gamma_{0,N-1}^{\{x_{0}\}}(s)
such that
\dist_{0,N-1}(X,\gamma)\leq\delta
.
Consider the discrete path
\xi\in\mathcal{X}^{N}
defined by
\xi_{n}=\gamma_{n}
for
0\leq n\leq N-1
.
Then, by construction,
\mathcal{A}_{N}(\xi)\leq\mathcal{S}_{0,N-1}(\gamma)\leq s
and
\dist_{N}((X_{n})_{0\leq n\leq N-1},\xi)\leq\delta
.
Thus,

(C.3.13)

\dist_{0,N-1}(X,\Gamma_{0,N-1}^{\{x_{0}\}}(s))<\delta\implies\dist_{N}((X_{n})_{0\leq n\leq N-1},\Gamma_{N}^{\{x_{0}\}}(s))\leq\delta\,.

Putting all together, we have that

\displaystyle\prob_{\!#1}\left(\dist_{N}((X_{n})_{0\leq n\leq N-1},\Gamma_{N}^{\{x_{0}\}}(s))>\delta\right)

\displaystyle\leq\prob_{x_{0}}\left(\dist_{0,N-1}(X,\Gamma_{0,N-1}^{\{x_{0}\}}(s)\geq\delta)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\prob_{x_{0}}\left(\dist_{0,N-1}(X,\Gamma_{0,N-1}^{\{x_{0}\}}(s)>\delta/2)\right)

(C.3.14)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{s-\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)

which concludes our proof.
∎

Finally, we end up with a large deviation principle on the discrete rescaled iterates
(x^{\eta}_{n})_{0\leq n\leq N-1}=(x_{n\lfloor\eta^{-1}\rfloor})_{0\leq n}
by leveraging
Lemma

C.4
.
In the following result, the functional
\eta^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{N}
is thus the action functional in
\mathcal{X}^{N}
of the process
(x^{\eta}_{n})_{0\leq n\leq N-1}
uniformly with respect to the starting point
x_{0}
in any compact set
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{X}
, as
\eta\to 0
.

Corollary C.1
.

Fix
N\geq 0
.
For any
s,\delta,\varepsilon>0
,
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{X}
compact, there exists
\eta_{0}>0
such that, for any
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0}]
, for any
x_{0}\in\mathcal{K}
,
\xi\in\Gamma_{N}^{\{x_{0}\}}(s)
, we have that

(C.3.15a)

\displaystyle\prob_{x_{0}}\left(\dist_{N}(x^{\eta},\xi)<\delta\right)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(-\frac{\mathcal{A}_{N}(\xi)+\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)

and

(C.3.15b)

\displaystyle\prob_{x_{0}}\left(\dist_{N}(x^{\eta},\Gamma_{N}^{\{x_{0}\}}(s))>\delta\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{s-\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,.

for all
\xi\in\Gamma_{N}^{\{x_{0}\}}(s)
.

Proof.

Fix
\delta>0
.
Choose
\eta_{0}
such that both
Proposition

C.2
and
Lemma

C.4
hold with
c\eta\leq\delta
.
Then for any
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0}]
, for any
x_{0}\in\mathcal{K}
,
\xi\in\Gamma_{N}^{\{x_{0}\}}(s)
,
\xi\in\Gamma_{N}^{\{x_{0}\}}(s)
, we have that

(C.3.16)

\displaystyle\prob_{x_{0}}\left(\dist_{N}((X_{n})_{0\leq n\leq N-1},\xi)<\delta\right)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(-\frac{\mathcal{A}_{N}(\xi)+\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,.

Now, if
\dist_{N}((X_{n})_{0\leq n\leq N-1},\xi)<\delta
and
\dist_{N}(x^{\eta},(X_{n})_{0\leq n\leq N-1})\leq\delta
, then
\dist_{N}(x^{\eta},\xi)<2\delta
.
Thus,

(C.3.17)

\displaystyle\prob_{\!#1}\left(\dist_{N}(x^{\eta},\xi)<2\delta\right)\geq\prob_{\!#1}\left(\dist_{N}((X_{n})_{0\leq n\leq N-1},\xi)<\delta\right)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(-\frac{\mathcal{A}_{N}(\xi)+\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,.

The second part can be obtained similarly.
∎

To summarize this part on large deviations principles, we state a corollary containing all the results that will be needed in the following sections.

Corollary C.2
.

Fix
N\geq 0
.
Then:

•

For all
s>0
, the set

(C.3.18)

\Gamma_{N}^{\mathcal{K}}(s)\coloneqq\{\xi\in\mathcal{X}^{N}:\xi_{0}\in\mathcal{K},\mathcal{A}_{N}(\xi)\leq s\}

is compact and
\mathcal{A}_{N}
is
lsc
on
\mathcal{X}^{N}
.

•

For all
s,\delta,\varepsilon>0
,
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{X}
compact, there exists
\eta_{0}>0
such that, for any
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0}]
, for all
x_{0}\in\mathcal{K}
,
n\leq N
,
\xi\in\Gamma_{n}^{\{x_{0}\}}(s)
, we have that

(C.3.19a)

\displaystyle\prob_{\!#1}\left(\dist_{n}(x^{\eta},\xi)<\delta\right)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(-\frac{\mathcal{A}_{n}(\xi)+\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)

and

(C.3.19b)

\displaystyle\prob_{\!#1}\left(\dist_{n}(x^{\eta},\Gamma_{n}^{\{x_{0}\}}(s))>\delta\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{s-\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,.

Appendix D
Attractors and limiting measures via large deviations

We now take inspiration from the framework of
Kifer [35]
in order to relate the sets of critical points to the sets where points can move at no cost.
Then, we relate the probability of
SGD
moving to neighborhoods of critical sets to the probability of being close to well-chosen paths, which enables us to use the results of the previous section.
Finally, we build upon these results to provide bounds on the limiting measure of
SGD
.

D.1.
Setup

We first need to define the gradient flow of
f
.

Definition 2
.

Define, for
x\in\mathcal{X}
, the flow
\Theta
of
-\nabla f
starting at
x
, i.e.,

(D.1.1)

\dot{\Theta}_{#1}(x)=-\nabla f(\Theta_{#1}(x))\quad\text{with}\quad\Theta_{#1}(x)=x

and let
F(x)
be the value of this flow at time
1
, i.e.,

(D.1.2)

F(x)=\Theta_{#1}(x)\,.

Lemma D.1

(Properties of the flow)
.

\Theta
is well-defined and continous in both time and space, and, for any
T\geq 0
,
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}([0,T],\mathcal{X})
such that
\gamma_{0}=x
,

(D.1.3)

\mathcal{S}_{0,T}(\gamma)=0\iff\gamma_{t}=\Theta_{#1}(x)~~\text{ for all }t\in[0,T]\,.

Proof.

The well-definition and continuity of
\Theta
are a consequence of
f
being twice continuously differentiable and of the global Cauchy-Lipschitz (Picard–Lindelöf) theorem for
ODE
(
ODE
).
The equivalence follows from the uniqueness of the flow and
Lemma

B.1
since

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{0,T}(\gamma)=0

\displaystyle\iff\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t},\dot{\gamma}_{t})=0\;\text{almost everywhere}

(D.1.4)

\displaystyle\iff\dot{\gamma}_{t}=-\nabla f(\gamma_{t})\;\text{almost everywhere}

and thus, by extending
\dot{\gamma}
by continuity, both
\gamma
and
\Theta
satisfy the same
ODE
with the same initial condition and are thus equal for all
t
by uniqueness of the solutions.
∎

The following lemma translates this for
F
.

Lemma D.2

(Properties of
F
)
.

F
is well-defined and continuous and, for any
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
,

(D.1.5)

\rho(x,x^{\prime})=0\iff x^{\prime}=F(x)\,.

Proof.

The implication
(\Leftarrow)
is immediate by definition of
F
and
Lemma

D.1
.
Now for the reverse, assume that
\rho(x,x^{\prime})=0
.
Following the proof of
Lemma

C.5
, there exists
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathcal{X})
such that
\gamma_{0}=x
,
\gamma_{1}=x^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{S}_{0,1}(\gamma)=0
.
By
Lemma

D.1
,
\gamma_{t}=\Theta_{#1}(x)
for all
t\in[0,1]
and thus
x^{\prime}=\Theta_{1}(x)=F(x)
.
∎

D.2.
Attractors

Let us first formalize the minimum-energy displacement between two points.

Definition 3

(
Kifer [35, §1.5]
)
.

Define, for
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle B(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{\mathcal{S}_{[0,T]}(\gamma):\gamma\in\mathcal{C}([0,T],\mathcal{X}),\gamma_{0}=x,\gamma_{T}=x^{\prime},T\in\mathbb{N},T\geq 1\right\}

(D.2.1)

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{\mathcal{A}_{N}(\xi):\xi\in\mathcal{X}^{N}\,,\,\xi_{0}=x\,,\,\xi_{N-1}=x^{\prime},\,N\geq 1\right\}\,.

The fact that these two expressions coincide directly come from the definition of
\rho
.

This enables us to define an equivalence relation for the critical points of
f
by grouping points connected by a null-energy path.

Proposition D.1
.

The relation
\sim
defined for any
x,x^{\prime}\in\crit f
as

(D.2.2)

x\sim x^{\prime}\iff B(x,x^{\prime})=B(x^{\prime},x)=0\,

is an equivalence relation on
\crit f
.

Proof.

•

Reflexivity:
B(x,x)=0
by
Lemma

D.1
since the flow started at
x\in\crit f
is constant.

•

Symmetry: this follows from the definition of
\sim
.

•

Transitivity: for any
x,x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\in\crit f
, we have by construction of
B
that

(D.2.3)

B(x,x^{\prime\prime})\leq B(x,x^{\prime})+B(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\,.

Therefore, if
x\sim x^{\prime}
and
x^{\prime}\sim x^{\prime\prime}
, then
B(x,x^{\prime\prime})=0
.

B(x^{\prime\prime},x)=0
follows with a symmetric argument and thus
x\sim x^{\prime\prime}
.
∎

Near critical points of
f
, the Lagrangian
\bar{\mathcal{L}}
is actually very regular.

Lemma D.3
.

For any
x\in\mathcal{X}
, there exists
\delta>0
such that
\bar{\mathcal{L}}
is finite and jointly Lipschitz continuous on
\mathbb{B}(x,\delta)\times\mathbb{B}(0,\delta)
.

Moreover, the following supremum is finite:

(D.2.4)

\sup\left\{\frac{\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},v)}{\lVert v\rVert^{2}}:x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}(x,\delta)\cap\crit f\,,v\in\mathbb{B}(0,\delta)\right\}<\infty\,.

Proof.

Take
x\in\mathcal{X}
.
We apply the implicit function theorem to the equation

(D.2.5)

\nabla_{v}\bar{\mathcal{H}}(x^{\prime},w)=v\,,

in the variables
(x^{\prime},v,w)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

We derive that

(D.2.6)

\nabla_{v}\bar{\mathcal{H}}(x^{\prime},v)=\frac{\ex\left[\mathsf{U}(x^{\prime},\omega)\exp\left(\langle v,\mathsf{U}(x^{\prime},\omega)\rangle\right)\right]}{\ex\left[\exp\left(\langle v,\mathsf{U}(x^{\prime},\omega)\rangle\right)\right]}

and thus
(x,0,0)
is solution of (
D.2.5
) since

(D.2.7)

\nabla_{v}\bar{\mathcal{H}}(x,0)=\ex\left[\mathsf{U}(x,\omega)\right]=0\,.

Moreover,
\Hess_{v}\bar{\mathcal{H}}(x,0)=\ex[\mathsf{U}(x,\omega)\mathsf{U}(x,\omega)^{\top}]
which is positive definite and thus invertible by the blanket assumptions.

Hence, we can apply the implicit function theorem to get that there exists
\delta>0
,
w\colon\mathbb{B}(x,\delta)\times\mathbb{B}(0,\delta)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}

\mathcal{C}^{2}
such that, for any
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}(x,\delta)
,
v\in\mathbb{B}(0,\delta)
,

(D.2.8)

\nabla_{v}\bar{\mathcal{H}}(x^{\prime},w(x^{\prime},v))=v\,.

Therefore, for any
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}(x,\delta)
,
v\in\mathbb{B}(0,\delta)
, since
\bar{\mathcal{L}}(x^{\prime},v)=\bar{\mathcal{H}}(x^{\prime},\cdot)^{*}(v)
, we have

(D.2.9)

\bar{\mathcal{L}}(x^{\prime},v)=\langle v,w(x^{\prime},v)\rangle-\bar{\mathcal{H}}(x^{\prime},w(x^{\prime},v))\,,

which is finite and
\mathcal{C}^{2}
on
\mathbb{B}(x,\delta)\times\mathbb{B}(0,\delta)
.
Therefore,
\bar{\mathcal{L}}
is actually
L
-jointly Lipschitz continuous on
\overline{\mathbb{B}}(x,\delta/2)\times\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,\delta/2)
.

For the second part of the lemma, note that the implicit function theorem also ensures that there is
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
a neighborhood of
0
such that,
for any
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}(x,\delta)
,
v\in\mathbb{B}(0,\delta)
,
w(x^{\prime},v)
is the unique solution of
Eq.

D.2.5
in
\mathcal{V}
.
But, for any
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}(x,\delta)
and
v=0
,
w=0
is a solution of
Eq.

D.2.5
in
\mathcal{V}
so that necessarily
w(x^{\prime},0)=0
, and, as a consequence,
\nabla_{v}\bar{\mathcal{L}}(x^{\prime},0)=0
.

Hence, for any
x^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathbb{B}}(x,\delta/2)
,
v\in\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,\delta/2)
,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{L}}(x^{\prime},v)

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathcal{L}}(x^{\prime},v)-\bar{\mathcal{L}}(x^{\prime},0)-\langle v,\nabla_{v}\bar{\mathcal{L}}(x^{\prime},0)\rangle

(D.2.10)

\displaystyle\leq{\frac{1}{2}}\sup_{\overline{\mathbb{B}}(x,\delta/2)\times\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,\delta/2)}\left\lVert\Hess_{v}\bar{\mathcal{L}}\right\rVert\lVert v\rVert^{2}\,.

To conclude, it suffices to note that, for any
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}(x,\delta)\cap\crit f
,
\nabla f(x)=0
and therefore
\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},\cdot)
and
\bar{\mathcal{L}}(x^{\prime},\cdot)
coincide.
∎

Lemma D.4
.

For any
x\in\crit(f)
, for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\delta>0
such that, for any
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}(x,\delta)
,
\rho(x,x^{\prime})\leq\epsilon
and
\rho(x^{\prime},x)\leq\epsilon
.

Proof.

Take
x\in\mathcal{X}
such that
\nabla f(x)=0
.
By
Lemma

D.3
, there exists
\delta>0
such that
\bar{\mathcal{L}}
is finite and
L
-jointly Lipschitz continuous on
\mathbb{B}(x,\delta)\times\mathbb{B}(0,\delta)
.
In particular, for any
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}(x,\delta)
,
v\in\mathbb{B}(0,\delta)
,

(D.2.11)

\bar{\mathcal{L}}(x^{\prime},v)\leq L\lVert v\rVert\,.

For any integer
k\geq 2
, by continuity of
\nabla f
(
Assumption

∗
), there is
0<\delta^{\prime}_{k}<\delta/k
such that, for every
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}(x,\delta^{\prime}_{k})
,
\lVert\nabla f(x^{\prime})\rVert\leq\delta/k
.
Then, for any
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}(x,\delta^{\prime}_{k})
,
v\in\mathbb{B}(0,\delta^{\prime}_{k})
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},v)

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathcal{L}}(x^{\prime},v+\nabla f(x^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq L\left(\lVert v+\nabla f(x^{\prime})\rVert\right)

\displaystyle\leq L\left(\lVert v\rVert+\lVert\nabla f(x^{\prime})\rVert\right)

(D.2.12)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2L\delta}{k}\,.

Take
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}(x,\delta^{\prime}_{k})
and define the path
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathcal{X})
by
\gamma_{t}=x+t(x^{\prime}-x)
.
Since for any
t\in[0,1]
,
\gamma_{t}\in\mathbb{B}(x,\delta^{\prime}_{k})
and
\dot{\gamma}_{t}=x^{\prime}-x\in\mathbb{B}(0,\delta^{\prime}_{k})
, we can bound the cost as

(D.2.13)

\displaystyle\rho(x,x^{\prime})\leq\mathcal{S}_{0,1}(\gamma)=\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t},\dot{\gamma}_{t})\>dt\leq\frac{2L\delta}{k}\,.

As a result, taking
k
large enough yields the existence of
\delta^{\prime}_{k}>0
such that, for any
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}(x,\delta^{\prime}_{k})
,
\rho(x,x^{\prime})\leq\epsilon
.
The reverse inequality is obtained by the same argument.
∎

The following lemma relates the Lagrangian to the gradient and our noise structure.

Lemma D.5
.

For any
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(D.2.14)

\mathcal{L}(x,v)\geq\frac{\lVert v+\nabla f(x)\rVert^{2}}{2\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x))}\,.

Proof.

For any
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have that

(D.2.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(x,v)

\displaystyle=-\langle v,\nabla f(x)\rangle+\bar{\mathcal{H}}(x,v)\leq-\langle v,\nabla f(x)\rangle+{\frac{1}{2}}\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x))\lVert v\rVert^{2}\,.

Taking the conjugate then yields that

(D.2.16)

\mathcal{L}(x,v)\geq\frac{\lVert v+\nabla f(x)\rVert^{2}}{2\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x))}\,.\qed

Now, let us define a potential function
U_{\infty}
on
\mathcal{X}
that uses the minimal displacement energy between two points that will be heavily used in the proofs.

Definition 4

(Potential)
.

Define, for
x\in\mathcal{X}

(D.2.17)

\displaystyle U_{\infty}(x)=2\alpha_{\infty}(f(x))

where
\alpha_{\infty}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbb{R}
is a twice continuously differentiable primitive of
1/\sigma_{\infty}^{2}
.

Lemma D.6
.

For any
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
,

(D.2.18)

U_{\infty}(x^{\prime})-U_{\infty}(x)\leq B(x,x^{\prime})\,.

Proof.

By
Definition

3
, there exists
T\geq 1
,
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}([0,T],\mathcal{X})
such that
\gamma_{0}=x
,
\gamma_{T}=x^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{S}_{[0,T]}(\gamma)\leq B(x,x^{\prime})+\varepsilon
.
Then, by Young’s inequality, we have that

\displaystyle U_{\infty}(x^{\prime})-U_{\infty}(x)

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\langle\dot{\gamma}_{t},\nabla f(\gamma_{t})\rangle}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(\gamma_{t}))}\>dt

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\leq\int_{0}^{T}\left(\frac{\langle\dot{\gamma}_{t},\nabla f(\gamma_{t})\rangle}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(\gamma_{t}))}+\frac{\lVert\nabla f(\gamma_{t})\rVert^{2}+\lVert\dot{\gamma}_{t}\rVert^{2}}{2\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(\gamma_{t}))}\right)\>dt}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\lVert\dot{\gamma}_{t}+\nabla f(\gamma_{t})\rVert^{2}}{2\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(\gamma_{t}))}\>dt

(D.2.19)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t},\dot{\gamma}_{t})\>dt=\mathcal{S}_{[0,T]}(\gamma)

where we used
Lemma

D.5
in the last inequality.
Finally, out choice of
\gamma
implies that

(D.2.20)

U_{\infty}(x^{\prime})-U_{\infty}(x)\leq B(x,x^{\prime})+\varepsilon

which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma D.7

(Equivalence classes are closed)
.

Equivalence classes of
\sim
are closed in
\mathcal{X}
. As a consequence, equivalence classes are compact.

Proof.

Let
x\in\crit f
, and take any sequence
(x^{\prime}_{k})_{k\geq 0}
in
\crit f
such that
x\sim x^{\prime}_{k}
for every
k\geq 0
and which converges to some
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
.
To show that the equivalence classes are closed, we need to show that
x\sim x^{\prime}
; then compacity follow directly since the equivalence classes are subsets of
\crit f
which is compact by assumption.

Since
\crit f
is closed, it holds that
x^{\prime}
belongs to
\crit f
.
We now show that both
B(x,x^{\prime})
and
B(x^{\prime},x)
are null.
We only show that
B(x,x^{\prime})=0
since the proof for the other equality is symmetric.

By
Lemma

D.4
, for any
\varepsilon>0
there is a neighborhood of
x^{\prime}
on which
\rho(\cdot,x^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon
.

Take
k
large enough so that
x^{\prime}_{k}
belongs to this neighborhood. Since
x\sim x^{\prime}_{k}
, there exists
N\geq 1
,
\xi\in(\mathcal{X})^{N}
such that
\xi_{0}=x
,
\xi_{N-1}=x^{\prime}_{k}
and
\mathcal{A}_{N}(\xi)\leq\varepsilon
.
Then, the path
\zeta\coloneqq(\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{N-1},x^{\prime})\in(\mathcal{X})^{N+1}
and satisfies
\zeta_{0}=x
,
\zeta_{N}=x^{\prime}
and

(D.2.21)

\mathcal{A}_{N+1}(\zeta){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=}\mathcal{A}_{N}(\xi)+\rho(x^{\prime}_{k},x^{\prime})\leq 2\varepsilon\,.

Hence, we have shown that, for any
\varepsilon>0
,
B(x,x^{\prime})\leq 2\varepsilon
so that
B(x,x^{\prime})=0
.
∎

Lemma D.8
.

For any set
\mathcal{C}\subset\crit f
and
x\in\mathcal{X}
, define

\displaystyle\rho(x,\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\inf_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}}\rho(x,x^{\prime})

(D.2.22)

\displaystyle\rho(\mathcal{C},x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\inf_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}}\rho(x^{\prime},x)\,.

Then, for any
\mathcal{C}\subset\crit f
and
r>0
, the set

(D.2.23)

\mathcal{W}_{r}(\mathcal{C})\coloneqq\{x\in\mathcal{X}:\rho(x,\mathcal{C})<r,\,\rho(\mathcal{C},x)<r\}

is a neighborhood of
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

Lemma

D.4
ensures that, for any
x\in\mathcal{C}
, there exists
\delta_{x}>0
such that, for any
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}(x,\delta_{x})
,
\rho(x,x^{\prime})<r
and
\rho(x^{\prime},x)<r
.
\mathcal{V}\coloneqq\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{B}(x,\delta_{x})
is then an open set that contains
\mathcal{C}
and is included in
\mathcal{W}_{r}(\mathcal{C})
.

∎

This lemma is adapted and significantly expanded from
Kifer [35, §1.5, Lem. 5.2]
to handle both the unboundedness of the space and the fact that
B
is neither
lsc
nor
usc
(
usc
).

Lemma D.9
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be an equivalence class of
\sim
. Then, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there is some
N\geq 1
such that, for any
x,z\in\mathcal{K}
,
there is
\xi\in(\mathcal{X})^{N}
such that
\xi_{0}=x
,
\xi_{N-1}=z
,
\mathcal{A}_{N}(\xi)<\varepsilon
and
\max_{0\leq n<N}d(\xi_{n},\mathcal{K})<\varepsilon
.

Proof.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
.

\mathcal{K}
is made of critical points of
f
so that by
Lemma

D.4
,

for any
x\in\mathcal{K}
, there exists
\delta_{x}>0
such that, for any
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}(x,\delta_{x})
,
\rho(x,x^{\prime})<\varepsilon
and
\rho(x^{\prime},x)<\varepsilon
.

Since the open sets
\mathbb{B}(x,\delta_{x})
for
x\in\mathcal{K}
form an open cover of
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{K}
is compact (
Lemma

D.7
), there exists a finite number of points
x_{i}\in\mathcal{K}
,
i\in I
such that

(D.2.24)

\mathcal{K}\subset\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathbb{B}(x_{i},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta_{x_{i}}})\,.

We first show that the result holds for the points
x_{i}
before explaining why it actually suffices for the general case.

Fix
i,j\in I
.
Since
x_{i}\sim x_{j}
,
B(x_{i},x_{j})=0
and therefore there exists sequences
\xi^{k}\in(\mathcal{X})^{N_{k}}
with
\xi^{k}_{0}=x_{i}
,
\xi^{k}_{N_{k}-1}=x_{j}
such that
\mathcal{A}_{N_{k}}(\xi^{k})\to 0
as
k\to\infty
.
For the sake of contradiction, assume that from some
k
, there always exists
0\leq n<N_{k}
such that
d(\xi^{k}_{n},\mathcal{K})\geq\varepsilon
. Define
n_{k}
as the smallest
n
such that it happens, which is necessarily greater or equal to
1
. Note that, by definition,
\xi^{k}_{n_{k}-1}
satisfies
d(\xi^{k}_{n_{k}-1},\mathcal{K})\leq\varepsilon
.

Define
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\coloneqq\{x\in\mathcal{X}:d(x,\mathcal{K})\leq\varepsilon\}
which is compact.
However, for
k
large enough,
\rho(\xi^{k}_{n_{k}-1},\xi^{k}_{n_{k}})\leq\mathcal{A}_{N_{k}}(\xi^{k})\leq 1
so that
(\xi^{k}_{n_{k}-1},\xi^{k}_{n_{k}})
belongs to
\Gamma_{2}^{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}(1)
, which is compact by
Corollary

C.2
.
Therefore, one can extract a subsequence from
(\xi^{k}_{n_{k}-1},\xi^{k}_{n_{k}},)_{k\geq 1}
that converges to some
(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}^{2}
that satisfies
d(z,\mathcal{K})\geq\varepsilon
.
Without loss of generality, at the cost of replacing the original sequence by the extracted subsequence, we assume that the sequence
(\xi^{k}_{n_{k}-1},\xi^{k}_{n_{k}})_{k\geq 1}
itself converges to
(x,z)
.

By
lsc
of
\rho
, one has that

\displaystyle\rho(x,z)

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\rho(\xi^{k}_{n_{k}-1},\xi^{k}_{n_{k}})

(D.2.25)

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{A}_{N_{k}}(\xi^{k})=0\,,

so that
\rho(x,z)=0
. We now show that
x=z
and that it is a critical point.
By
Lemma

D.6
, we have that

(D.2.26a)

\displaystyle U_{\infty}(\xi^{k}_{n_{k}-1})-U_{\infty}(x_{i})

\displaystyle\leq B(x_{i},\xi^{k}_{n_{k}-1})\leq\mathcal{A}_{N_{k}}(\xi^{k})

(D.2.26b)

\displaystyle U_{\infty}(x_{j})-U_{\infty}(\xi^{k}_{n_{k}})

\displaystyle\leq B(x_{j},\xi^{k}_{n_{k}})\leq\mathcal{A}_{N_{k}}(\xi^{k})\,,

so, taking the limit
k\to\infty
yields

(D.2.27a)

\displaystyle U_{\infty}(x)-U_{\infty}(x_{i})

\displaystyle\leq 0

(D.2.27b)

\displaystyle U_{\infty}(x_{j})-U_{\infty}(z)

\displaystyle\leq 0\,.

However, the fact that
x_{i}
and
x_{j}
are equivalent and
Lemma

D.6
imply that
U_{\infty}(x_{i})=U_{\infty}(x_{j})
so that
U_{\infty}(x)\leq U_{\infty}(z)
. Since
\alpha_{\infty}
is increasing, we have that
f(x)\leq f(z)
.

But we have that
\rho(x,z)=0
so that
z=\Theta_{1}(x)
. Therefore, if
\nabla f(x)\neq 0
, we would have

(D.2.28)

f(z)-f(x)=-\int_{0}^{1}\lVert\nabla f(\Theta_{t}(x)\rVert^{2}\>dt<0\,,

which would be a contradiction. Therefore,
\nabla f(x)=0
and
x=z
.
Since
x
is a critical point, to show that it belongs to
\mathcal{K}
, it suffices to show that
x_{i}\sim x
.

Take
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}}>0
.
By
Lemma

D.8
,

\mathcal{W}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}}(\{x\})
is
a neighborhood
of
x
.
Since
\xi^{k}_{n_{k}}
converges to
x
, for
k
large enough,
\xi^{k}_{n_{k}}
belongs to
\mathcal{W}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}}(\{x\})
.

(\xi^{k}_{0},\dots,\xi^{k}_{n_{k}},x)
and
(x,\xi^{k}_{n_{k}},\dots,\xi^{k}_{N_{k}-1})
are, respectively, paths from
x_{i}
to
x
and from
x
to
x_{j}
with action cost of at most
\mathcal{A}_{N_{k}}(\xi^{k})+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}}
so that, for
k
large enough,
B(x_{i},x)\leq 2{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}}
and
B(x,x_{j})\leq 2{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}}
.

Hence, we have shown that
B(x_{i},x)=B(x,x_{j})=0
.
Then, we also have

(D.2.29)

0\leq B(x,x_{i})\leq B(x,x_{j})+B(x_{j},x_{i})=0\,,

and therefore
x\sim x_{i}
and thus
x
belongs to
\mathcal{K}
. This is a contradiction.

Therefore, there must exist some sequence
\xi^{i,j}\in(\mathcal{X})^{N_{i,j}}
with
\xi^{i,j}_{0}=x_{i}
,
\xi^{i,j}_{N_{i,j}-1}=x_{j}
such that
\mathcal{A}_{N_{i,j}}(\xi^{i,j})<\varepsilon
and
\max_{0\leq n<N_{i,j}}d(\xi^{i,j}_{n},\mathcal{K})<\varepsilon
.

Finally, for the general class, consider
x,z\in\mathcal{K}
. By
Eq.

D.2.24
, there exists
i,j\in I
such that
x\in\mathbb{B}(x_{i},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta_{x_{i}}})
,
z\in\mathbb{B}(x_{j},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta_{x_{j}}})
.

Consider
\xi\in(\mathcal{X})^{N_{i,j}+2}
a
extension of
\xi^{i,j}
defined by

(D.2.30)

\xi_{n}=\begin{cases}x&\quad if $n=0$,\\
\xi^{i,j}_{n-1}&\quad if $0<n\leq N_{i,j}$,\\
z&\quad if $n=N_{i,j}+1$,
\,.\end{cases}

that still satisfies
\max_{0\leq n<N_{i,j}+2}d(\xi_{n},\mathcal{K})<\varepsilon
.
Then, one has that

(D.2.31)

\mathcal{A}_{N_{i,j}+2}(\xi)<\mathcal{A}_{N_{i,j}}(\xi^{i,j})+2\varepsilon\leq 3\varepsilon\,,

which concludes the proof.

∎

The following lemma is inspired by
Alongi & Nelson [1, Prop. 3.3.11]
.

Lemma D.10
.

Equivalence classes are connected.

Proof.

Fix
\mathcal{K}
an equivalence class of
\sim
.

For the sake of contradiction, assume that there are
\mathcal{U}
,
\mathcal{V}
disjoint open sets of
\mathcal{X}
such that both
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{K}
are non-empty and
\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{U}\cup\mathcal{V})\cap\mathcal{K}
.

Take
x\in\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{K}
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{K}
.
By
Lemma

D.9
, there exists a sequence of paths
\xi^{k}\in(\mathcal{X})^{N_{k}}
with
\xi^{k}_{0}=x
,
\xi^{k}_{N_{k}-1}=x^{\prime}
for
k\geq 0
such that
\mathcal{A}_{N_{k}}(\xi^{k})\to 0
as
k\to\infty
and
\max_{0\leq n<N_{k}}d(\xi^{k}_{n},\mathcal{K})\to 0
as
k\to\infty
.
Define
a_{k}
as the last point of
\xi^{k}
that belongs to
\mathcal{U}
and
b_{k}
as the successor of
a_{k}
in
\xi^{k}
. Formally,
a_{k}
and
b_{k}
are defined by
a_{k}=\xi^{k}_{i_{k}}
and
b_{k}=\xi^{k}_{i_{k}+1}
where
i_{k}=\max\{i<N_{k}:\xi^{k}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}\}
.

By construction, both
d(a_{k},\mathcal{K})
and
d(b_{k},\mathcal{K})
go to zero as
k\to\infty
. In particular, both sequences lie in
\mathcal{U}_{1}(\mathcal{K})
from some point onward, which is relatively compact, so they admit convergent subsequences. Without loss of generality, thus assume that
a_{k}\to a
and
b_{k}\to b
as
k\to\infty
and
a
,
b
belong to
\mathcal{K}
.

Since
a_{k}
belongs to
\mathcal{U}
for all
k\geq 0
,
a_{k}
is never in
\mathcal{V}
so that
a
does not belong to
\mathcal{V}
either. Since
a
belongs to
\mathcal{K}
, it must thus belong to
\mathcal{U}
. Similarly,
b
must belong to
\mathcal{V}
.

However, by construction, we have that
\rho(a_{k},b_{k})\leq\mathcal{A}_{N_{k}}(\xi^{k})\to 0
as
k\to\infty
so that
\rho(a_{k},b_{k})
converges to
0
as
k\to\infty
as well. By
lsc
of
\rho
(
Corollary

C.2
with
N=1
),
\rho(a,b)=0
so that
b=F(a)
by
Lemma

D.2
. But since
a
belongs to
\mathcal{K}
, it is a critical point of
f
and therefore
a=F(a)
. Hence
a=b
with
a\in\mathcal{U}
and
b\in\mathcal{V}
, which is a contradiction.
∎

Lemma D.11
.

Any connected component of
\crit f
is included in a single equivalence class.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a connected component of
\crit f
and fix
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}
.
We begin by considering a stronger version of
Assumption

1
Item

(c.5pt)
, namely that there exists
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathcal{K})
absolutely continuous such that
\gamma_{0}=x
,
\gamma_{1}=x^{\prime}
, i.e., such that
\gamma
is differentiable almost everywhere with
\int_{0}^{1}\lVert\dot{\gamma}_{t}\rVert\>dt<\infty
. We show that
x\sim x^{\prime}
, i.e., that
B(x,x^{\prime})=B(x^{\prime},x)=0
.

Let us begin by showing that
B(x,x^{\prime})=0
.

Since
\crit f
is compact and
\mathcal{K}
is closed as connected component of a closed set,
\mathcal{K}
is compact. By invoking
Lemma

D.3
at every point of
\mathcal{K}
and extracting a finite covering from the family of balls obtained, we have that, there exists
\delta>0
,
L>0
such that, for every
x\in\mathcal{K}
,
v\in\mathbb{B}(0,\delta)
,

(D.2.32)

\mathcal{L}(x,v)\leq L{\lVert v\rVert^{2}}\,.

Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,\delta)
and define, for any
t\in[0,1]
,

(D.2.33)

\lambda_{t}\coloneqq\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\max(\lVert\dot{\gamma}_{s}\rVert,1)}{\varepsilon}\>ds\,.

We have that
\lambda
is an increasing bijection from
[0,1]
to
[0,\lambda_{1}]
and is absolutely continuous with
\dot{\lambda}_{t}={\max(\lVert\dot{\gamma}_{t}\rVert,1)}/{\varepsilon}
almost everywhere.
Consider
\tau\colon[0,\lambda_{1}]\to[0,1]
the inverse of
\lambda
, which is absolutely continuous with
\dot{\tau}_{t}={\varepsilon}/{\max(\lVert\dot{\gamma}_{\tau_{t}}\rVert,1)}
almost everywhere.
Define
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}([0,\lambda_{1}],\mathcal{X})
by
\varphi_{t}=\gamma_{\tau_{t}}
for any
t\in[0,\lambda_{1}]
.
Then,
\varphi
is absolutely continuous and, for any
t\in[0,\lambda_{1}]
,

(D.2.34)

\dot{\varphi}_{t}=\dot{\gamma}_{\tau_{t}}\dot{\tau}_{t}=\frac{\varepsilon\dot{\gamma}_{\tau_{t}}}{\max(\lVert\dot{\gamma}_{\tau_{t}}\rVert,1)}\,

which has norm less than
\varepsilon<\delta
.

Therefore, we have that,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{0,\lambda_{1}}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\lambda_{1}}\mathcal{L}(\varphi_{t},\dot{\varphi}_{t})\>dt

\displaystyle\leq L\int_{0}^{\lambda_{1}}\lVert\dot{\varphi}_{t}\rVert^{2}\>dt

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon L\int_{0}^{\lambda_{1}}\lVert\dot{\varphi}_{t}\rVert\>dt

\displaystyle=\varepsilon L\int_{0}^{\lambda_{1}}\dot{\tau}_{t}\lVert\dot{\gamma}_{\tau_{t}}\rVert\>dt

(D.2.35)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon L\int_{0}^{1}\lVert\dot{\gamma}_{t}\rVert\>dt

where the last equality is obtained by the change of variable
t\leftarrow\tau_{t}
.
Thus, we have shown that, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\delta)
,

(D.2.36)

B(x,x^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon L\int_{0}^{1}\lVert\dot{\gamma}_{t}\rVert\>dt

with
\int_{0}^{1}\lVert\dot{\gamma}_{t}\rVert\>dt<\infty
by construction so that
B(x,x^{\prime})=0
.

Reversing the roles of
x
and
x^{\prime}
and considering the path
(\gamma_{1-t})_{t\in[0,1]}
then yields that
B(x^{\prime},x)=0
.
Therefore, we have shown that if there exists
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathcal{K})
absolutely continuous such that
\gamma_{0}=x
,
\gamma_{1}=x^{\prime}
, then
x\sim x^{\prime}
.

We now relax our assumption on the paths from absolute continuity to piecewise absolute continuity (
Assumption

∗
).
For
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}
, by assumption, there exists
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathcal{K})
such that
\gamma_{0}=x
,
\gamma_{1}=x^{\prime}
and such that it is piecewise absolutely continuous:
\gamma
is differentiable almost everywhere and there exists
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{N}=1
such that
\dot{\gamma}
is integrable on every closed interval of
(t_{n},t_{n+1})
for
n=0,\dots,N-1
.
Take
0\leq n<N-1
and
t_{n}<s<t<t_{n+1}
.

\gamma
restricted to
[s,t]
is absolutely continuous so that, by the previous case, all the points of
\{\gamma_{u}:u\in[s,t]\}
are included in a single equivalence class
\mathcal{K}
.
Taking
s\to t_{n}
and
t\to t_{n+1}
yields that
\{\gamma_{u}:u\in(t_{n},t_{n+1})\}
is included in
\mathcal{K}
.
Moreover, by continuity of
\gamma
,
\gamma_{t_{n}}
and
\gamma_{t_{n+1}}
belong to the closure of
\mathcal{K}
, which is closed by
Lemma

D.7
, so that
\gamma_{t_{n}}
and
\gamma_{t_{n+1}}
belong to
\mathcal{K}
as well.
Therefore,
\gamma_{t_{n}}\sim\gamma_{t_{n+1}}
.
By transitivity, we obtain that
x=\gamma_{0}\sim\gamma_{t_{1}}\sim\dots\sim\gamma_{t_{N-1}}\sim\gamma_{1}=x^{\prime}
so that
x\sim x^{\prime}
.
∎

Combining
Lemmas

D.10
and
D.11
, we have shown that any connected component of
\crit f
is included in a single equivalence class and since they are connected, two distinct connected components of
\crit f
cannot belong to the same equivalent class; hence, we have that they coincide.

Corollary D.1
.

The equivalence classes of
\sim
are exactly connected components of
\crit f
.

We end this section by providing a sufficient condition for
B(x,x^{\prime})
to be finite.

Lemma D.12
.

Consider
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
and assume that there exists
T>0
,
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}([0,T],\mathcal{X})
absolutely continuous
such that
\gamma_{0}=x
,
\gamma_{T}=x^{\prime}
and such that, for every
t\in[0,T]
,
\nabla f(\gamma_{t})
is in the interior of the closed convex hull of the support of
\mathsf{U}(\gamma_{t},\omega)
, i.e.,

(D.2.37)

\nabla f(\gamma_{t})\in\intr\clconv\supp\mathsf{U}(\gamma_{t},\omega)\,.

Then,
B(x,x^{\prime})<\infty
.

Proof.

First, fix
t\in[0,T]
and consider the exponential family of distributions

(D.2.38)

\propto e^{\langle p,\mathsf{U}(\gamma_{t},\omega)\rangle}\prob({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}\omega)

with parameter
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We begin by applying
Brown [7, Thm. 3.6]
. The family above is indeed minimal in the sense of
Brown [7, §1.1]
since the interior of
\clconv\supp\mathsf{U}(\gamma_{t},\omega)
is non-empty by assumption.
Combined with
Eq.

D.2.37
,
Brown [7, Thm. 3.6]

then implies that, for every
t\in[0,T]
,
\nabla f(\gamma_{t})
belongs to
\nabla_{p}\bar{\mathcal{H}}(\gamma_{t},\mathcal{X})
so there exists
p_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\nabla f(\gamma_{t})=\nabla_{p}\bar{\mathcal{H}}(\gamma_{t},p_{t})
.
As in the proof of
Lemma

D.3
, we will now invoke the implicit function theorem on the equation

(D.2.39)

\nabla_{p}\bar{\mathcal{H}}(x,p)=\nabla f(x)+v\,.

For this, need to check that
\Hess_{p}\bar{\mathcal{H}}(\gamma_{t},p_{t})
is positive definite.
For notational convenience, denote by
\ex_{p_{t}}
,
\cov_{p_{t}}
and
\var_{p_{t}}
the expectation, covariance and variance operators with respect to the reweighted distribution

(D.2.40)

\propto e^{\langle p_{t},\mathsf{U}(\gamma_{t},\omega)\rangle}\prob(d\omega)\,.

For any non-zero vector
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, computing the Hessian of
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
at
(\gamma_{t},p_{t})
yields that

(D.2.41)

\displaystyle\langle u,\Hess_{p}\bar{\mathcal{H}}(\gamma_{t},p_{t})u\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle u,\cov_{p_{t}}\left[\mathsf{U}(\gamma_{t},\omega)\right]u\rangle

(D.2.42)

\displaystyle=\var_{p_{t}}\left[\langle u,\mathsf{U}(\gamma_{t},\omega)\rangle\right]

(D.2.43)

\displaystyle=\min_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\ex_{p_{t}}\left[\left(\langle u,\mathsf{U}(\gamma_{t},\omega)\rangle-\alpha\right)^{2}\right]

(D.2.44)

\displaystyle=\min_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\frac{\ex\left[\left(\langle u,\mathsf{U}(\gamma_{t},\omega)\rangle-\alpha\right)^{2}e^{\langle p_{t},\mathsf{U}(\gamma_{t},\omega)\rangle}\right]}{\ex\left[e^{\langle p_{t},\mathsf{U}(\gamma_{t},\omega)\rangle}\right]}\,.

The blanket assumptions on the noise (
Section

B.2
) and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality
then yield

(D.2.45)

\displaystyle\langle u,\Hess_{p}\bar{\mathcal{H}}(\gamma_{t},p_{t})u\rangle

\displaystyle\geq e^{-2\lVert p_{t}\rVert M\left(1+\lVert\gamma_{t}\rVert\right)}\min_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\ex\left[\left(\langle u,\mathsf{U}(\gamma_{t},\omega)\rangle-\alpha\right)^{2}\right]

(D.2.46)

\displaystyle=e^{-2\lVert p_{t}\rVert M\left(1+\lVert\gamma_{t}\rVert\right)}\langle u,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cov}\left[\mathsf{U}(\gamma_{t},\omega)\right]u\rangle\,,

which is positive by the positive definiteness of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cov}\left[\mathsf{U}(\gamma_{t},\omega)\right]
(
Section

B.2
).
Hence,
\Hess_{p}\bar{\mathcal{H}}(\gamma_{t},p_{t})
is positive definite and we can apply the implicit function theorem to
Eq.

D.2.39
to obtain that there exists
\delta(\gamma_{t})>0
,
p\colon\mathbb{B}(\gamma_{t},\delta(\gamma_{t}))\times\mathbb{B}(0,\delta(\gamma_{t}))\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that,
for any
x\in\mathbb{B}(\gamma_{t},\delta(\gamma_{t}))
,
v\in\mathbb{B}(0,\delta(\gamma_{t}))
,

(D.2.47)

\nabla_{p}\bar{\mathcal{H}}(x,p(x,v))=\nabla f(x)+v\,,

or, equivalently

(D.2.48)

\nabla_{p}\mathcal{H}(x,p(x,v))=v\,.

Therefore, as in the proof of
Lemma

D.3
, we obtain that
\mathcal{L}
is continuous on
\overline{\mathbb{B}}(\gamma_{t},\delta(\gamma_{t})/2)\times\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,\delta(\gamma_{t})/2)
and therefore bounded by
M(\gamma_{t})>0
.
Since
\gamma
is continuous,
\left\{\gamma_{t}:t\in[0,T]\right\}
is compact and therefore, by extracting a finite covering from

(D.2.49)

\bigcup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{B}(\gamma_{t},\delta(\gamma_{t})/2)\,,

we obtain that there exists
\delta>0
and
M>0
such that, for every
t\in[0,T]
,
\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{t},\cdot)
is finite and bounded by
M
on
\mathbb{B}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0},\delta)
.
Choosing a
\varphi
reparametrization of
\gamma
, which is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, such that
\lVert\dot{\varphi}_{t}\rVert<\delta
for every
t\in[0,S]
, we thus obtain a path
such that

(D.2.50)

\mathcal{S}_{0,S}(\varphi)=\int_{0}^{S}\mathcal{L}(\varphi_{t},\dot{\varphi}_{t})\>dt\leq MS<\infty\,,

which implies that
B(x,x^{\prime})<\infty
.
∎

D.3.
Lyapunov condition

Definition 5

(Stopping times for the accelerated process)
.

For any set
S\subset\mathcal{X}
, we define the hitting and exit times of
S
:

(D.3.1a)

\displaystyle\sigma_{S}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\inf\{n\geq 1:x^{\eta}_{n}\in S\}

(D.3.1b)

\displaystyle\tau_{S}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\inf\{n\geq 0:x^{\eta}_{n}\notin S\}\,.

We will need the following concentration lemma and its corollary.

Lemma D.13

(Part of the proof of
[
61
, Th. 1.19]
)
.

Let
X
be a random variable in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that, for all
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(D.3.2)

\log\ex\left[\exp\left(\langle v,X\rangle\right)\right]\leq\frac{\lVert v\rVert^{2}}{2}\,.

Then, for all
t>0
, we have

(D.3.3)

\prob\left(\lVert X\rVert^{2}\geq t\right)\leq 6^{d}\exp\left(-\frac{t}{8}\right)\,.

Corollary D.2
.

In the context of
Lemma

D.13
, it holds that

(D.3.4)

\ex\left[\lVert X\rVert^{2}\right]\leq 16d\log 6\,.

Proof.

Since
\lVert X\rVert^{2}
is non-negative, its expectation can be written as

\displaystyle\ex\left[\lVert X\rVert^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\prob\left(\lVert X\rVert^{2}>t\right)\>dt

(D.3.5)

\displaystyle\leq 8d\log 6+\int_{8d\log 6}^{+\infty}\prob\left(\lVert X\rVert^{2}>t\right)\>dt\,.

Invoking
Lemma

D.13
then yields that

(D.3.6)

\ex\left[\lVert X\rVert^{2}\right]\leq 8d\log 6+6^{d}\int_{8d\log 6}^{+\infty}\exp\left(-\frac{t}{8}\right)\>dt\leq 8d\log 6+8\,,

which concludes the proof since
1\leq d\log 6
.
∎

Lemma D.14

(Lyapunov condition)
.

Define
U_{\infty}
as in
Lemma

D.6
.
Then, there exists
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{X}
compact,
\eta_{0}>0
,
c>0
such that, for any
\eta\leq\eta_{0}
,
n\geq 0
, if
x_{n}\notin\mathcal{K}
, then, almost surely,

\displaystyle U_{\infty}(x_{n+1})-U_{\infty}(x_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\eta\left(\frac{\lVert\mathsf{U}(x_{n},\omega_{n})\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x_{n}))}-\frac{\lVert\nabla f(x_{n})\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x_{n}))}\right)

(D.3.7)

\displaystyle\leq\eta\left(\frac{\lVert\mathsf{U}(x_{n},\omega_{n})\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x_{n}))}-(16d\log 6+c)\right)\,.

Proof.

By
Assumption

∗∗
, there is
R\geq{\frac{1}{2}}
,
c>0
such that, for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
such that
\lVert x\rVert\geq R
,

(D.3.8)

\begin{cases}f(x)\geq c\\
c\leq\frac{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x))}{\lVert x\rVert^{s}}\leq c^{-1}\\
\frac{\lVert\nabla f(x)\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x))}\geq 16d\log 6+c\,.\end{cases}

Then, define

\mathcal{K}\coloneqq\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,2R+1)
.

By definition,
U_{\infty}
is twice continuously differentiable and, its Hessian satisfies, for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
,

(D.3.9)

\Hess U_{\infty}(x)\preccurlyeq\frac{2\Hess f(x)}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x))}\preccurlyeq\frac{2\beta(f)}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x))}I\,.

For the sake of clarity, for any
n\geq 0
, denote by
\delta x_{n}
the quantity

(D.3.10)

\delta x_{n}=\frac{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}{\eta}\,.

For any
n\geq 0
, we now have

\displaystyle U_{\infty}(x_{n+1})-U_{\infty}(x_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\eta\langle\nabla U_{\infty}(x_{n}),\delta x_{n}\rangle

(D.3.11)

\displaystyle+\frac{\eta^{2}}{2}\lVert\delta x_{n}\rVert^{2}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\frac{2\beta(f)}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x_{n}+t(x_{n+1}-x_{n})))}\,.

We first focus on bounding the last term. First note that, by the blanket assumptions,

\lVert x_{n+1}-x_{n}\rVert\leq 2\eta M(1+\lVert x_{n}\rVert)
so that,
For any
t\in[0,1]
,
\eta\leq(4M)^{-1}
,

\displaystyle\lVert x_{n}+t(x_{n+1}-x_{n})\rVert

\displaystyle\geq\lVert x_{n}\rVert-\lVert x_{n+1}-x_{n}\rVert

\displaystyle\geq\lVert x_{n}\rVert-2\eta M(1+\lVert x_{n}\rVert)

(D.3.12)

\displaystyle\geq{\frac{1}{2}}(\lVert x_{n}\rVert-1)\,.

If
x_{n}
is outside of
\mathcal{K}
, we have that
\lVert x_{n}\rVert\geq 2R+1
and thus
\lVert x_{n}+t(x_{n+1}-x_{n})\rVert\geq R
.
Moreover, since
R\geq{\frac{1}{2}}
,
x_{n}
being outside of
\mathcal{K}
also implies that
{\frac{1}{2}}\lVert x_{n}\rVert\geq 1
and so
\lVert x_{n}+t(x_{n+1}-x_{n})\rVert\geq\tfrac{1}{4}\lVert x_{n}\rVert
.
By the definition of
R
, we thus have

\displaystyle\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x_{n}+t(x_{n+1}-x_{n})))

\displaystyle\geq c\lVert x_{n}+t(x_{n+1}-x_{n})\rVert^{s}

\displaystyle\geq c\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{s}\lVert x_{n}\rVert^{s}

(D.3.13)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{c^{2}}{4^{s}}\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x_{n}))\,.

Thus, if
x_{n}\notin\mathcal{K}
,
Eq.

D.3.9
yields that

(D.3.14)

U_{\infty}(x_{n+1})-U_{\infty}(x_{n})\leq\eta\langle\nabla U_{\infty}(x_{n}),\delta x_{n}\rangle+\eta^{2}\frac{4^{s}\beta(f)}{c^{2}\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x_{n}))}\lVert\delta x_{n}\rVert^{2}\,.

Now, we can rewrite the inner product as

\displaystyle\langle\nabla U_{\infty}(x_{n}),\delta x_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{2\langle\nabla f(x_{n}),\delta x_{n}\rangle}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x_{n}))}

(D.3.15)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lVert\delta x_{n}+\nabla f(x_{n})\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x_{n}))}-\frac{\lVert\nabla f(x_{n})\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x_{n}))}-\frac{\lVert\delta x_{n}\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x_{n}))}\,.

Plugging this into
Eq.

D.3.14
and assuming that
\eta\leq\frac{c^{2}}{4^{s}\beta(f)}
, we obtain

\displaystyle U_{\infty}(x_{n+1})-U_{\infty}(x_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\eta\left(\frac{\lVert\delta x_{n}+\nabla f(x_{n})\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x_{n}))}-\frac{\lVert\nabla f(x_{n})\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x_{n}))}\right)

(D.3.16)

\displaystyle\leq\eta\left(\frac{\lVert\mathsf{U}(x_{n},\omega_{n})\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x_{n}))}-\frac{\lVert\nabla f(x_{n})\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x_{n}))}\right)

where we unrolled
\delta x_{n}
in the last inequality.
Using again that
x_{n}\notin\mathcal{K}
, we obtain

(D.3.17)

U_{\infty}(x_{n+1})-U_{\infty}(x_{n})\leq\eta\left(\frac{\lVert\mathsf{U}(x_{n},\omega_{n})\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x_{n}))}-(16d\log 6+c)\right)

which concludes the proof.
∎

We will reuse, in later sections, the following fact that we thus state as a lemma: the sequence of iterates of
SGD
is
(weak) Feller
(see e.g.,
[
24
, Def. 4.4.2]
).

Lemma D.15
.

The Markov chain
(x_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is
(
weak
)
Feller.

Proof.

since both
\nabla f
and
\mathsf{U}
are continuous, for any
g\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
continuous and bounded, the function.

(D.3.18)

x\in\mathcal{X}\longmapsto\ex_{x}\left[g\left(x_{1}\right)\right]=\ex_{x}\left[g\left(x-\eta\nabla f(x)+\eta\mathsf{U}(x,\omega)\right)\right]

is still continuous and bounded. Therefore, the Markov chain
(x_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is weak-Feller.
∎

Lemma D.16
.

There is
\eta_{0}>0
such that, for any
\eta\leq\eta_{0}
, there exists an invariant probability measure for
(x_{n})_{n\geq 0}
.

Proof.

We invoke a general result on weak-Feller Markov chains that satisfy a Lyapunov condition, e.g.,
Hernández-Lerma & Lasserre [24, Thm. 7.2.4]
or
Douc et al. [14, Thm. 12.3.6]
.

First,
Lemma

D.15
ensures that
(x_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is weak-Feller.

Moreover, by
Lemma

D.14
, there exists
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{X}
compact,
\eta_{0}>0
,
c>0
such that, for any
\eta\leq\eta_{0}
,
x_{0}=x\notin\mathcal{K}
,

(D.3.19)

U_{\infty}(x_{1})-U_{\infty}(x)\leq\eta\left(\frac{\lVert\mathsf{U}(x,\omega_{0})\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x))}-(16d\log 6+c)\right)\,.

Passing to the expectation yields that, for any
x\notin\mathcal{K}
,

(D.3.20)

\ex_{x}\left[U_{\infty}(x_{1})\right]-{U_{\infty}(x)}\leq\eta\left(\frac{\ex_{x}\left[\lVert\mathsf{U}(x,\omega_{0})\rVert^{2}\right]}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x))}-(16d\log 6+c)\right)\,.

Appplying
Corollary

D.2
with
X\leftarrow\frac{\mathsf{U}(x,\omega_{0})}{\sqrt{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x))}}
(the conditions of application are verified from
Assumption

∗
Item

(c.5pt)
) yields that

(D.3.21)

\ex_{x}\left[U_{\infty}(x_{1})\right]-{U_{\infty}(x)}\leq-\eta c\,.

Hence, for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
, it holds

(D.3.22)

\ex_{x}\left[U_{\infty}(x_{1})\right]-{U_{\infty}(x)}\leq-\eta c+\one\{x\in\mathcal{K}\}\left(\sup_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}}\ex_{x^{\prime}}[U_{\infty}(x^{\prime})]-\inf_{\mathcal{X}}U_{\infty}+\eta c\right)\,,

with
U_{\infty}
which is not identically equal to its minimum since
f
is coercive.

Therefore, we can apply
Hernández-Lerma & Lasserre [24, Thm. 7.2.4]
with
U_{\infty}-\inf_{\mathcal{X}}U_{\infty}
, that guarantees that there exists an invariant probability measure for
(x_{n})_{n\geq 0}
.
∎

Lemma D.17
.

There exists a compact set
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{X}
,
\eta_{0}>0
, such that for any compact set
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{X}
such that
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, there exists
a,b>0
, such that

(D.3.23)

\forall\eta\leq\eta_{0}\,,\,x\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{D}\,,\,n\geq 0\,,\,\prob_{x}\left(\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}>n\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{an}{\eta}+\frac{b}{\eta}\right)\,.

Proof.

This result is a consequence of
Lemma

D.14
: there exists
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{X}
compact,
\eta_{0}>0
,
c>0
such that, for any
\eta\leq\eta_{0}
,
n\geq 0
, if
x_{n}\notin\mathcal{K}
, then, almost surely,

(D.3.24)

U_{\infty}(x_{n+1})-U_{\infty}(x_{n})\leq\eta\left(\frac{\lVert\mathsf{U}(x_{n},\omega_{n})\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x_{n}))}-(16d\log 6+c)\right)\,.

First, let us choose
\mathcal{D}
. There is some
R>0
such that
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{B}(0,R)
. Define
\widetilde{R}\coloneqq e^{4M+1}(R+1)
and
\mathcal{D}\coloneqq\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,\widetilde{R})
.
With
\eta\leq(4M)^{-1}
, if, for some
n\geq 0
,
x_{n}
is not in
\mathcal{D}
, by
Lemma

B.3
, for any
k\leq\lceil\eta^{-1}\rceil
,
x_{n+k}
has norm greater or equal to
R
and thus
x_{n+k}
is not in
\mathcal{K}
either.

Now, fix
N\geq 0
and consider the event
\left\{\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}>N\right\}
with
x_{0}=x\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{D}
. This means that, for any
0\leq n\leq N
,
x^{\eta}_{n}
is outside of
\mathcal{D}
and so, for any
0\leq n\leq N\lfloor\eta^{-1}\rfloor
,
x_{n}
is not in
\mathcal{K}
.

Summing
Eq.

D.3.24
over
n=0,\dots,N\lfloor\eta^{-1}\rfloor-1
yields

\displaystyle U_{\infty}(x^{\eta}_{N})-U_{\infty}(x_{0})

\displaystyle=U_{\infty}(x_{N\lfloor\eta^{-1}\rfloor})-U_{\infty}(x_{0})

(D.3.25)

\displaystyle\leq\eta\sum_{n=0}^{N\lfloor\eta^{-1}\rfloor-1}\left(\frac{\lVert\mathsf{U}(x_{n},\omega_{n})\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x_{n}))}-(16d\log 6+c)\right)\,.

Define
\Delta\coloneqq\sup_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{D}}U_{\infty}-\inf_{\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{D}}U_{\infty}
which is finite since
f
is coercive. By definition, we have that
U_{\infty}(x^{\eta}_{N})-U_{\infty}(x_{0})\geq-\Delta
.

Therefore, on the event
\left\{\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}>N\right\}
, we have

(D.3.26)

\sum_{n=0}^{N\lfloor\eta^{-1}\rfloor-1}{\frac{\lVert\mathsf{U}(x_{n},\omega_{n})\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}\circ f(x_{n})}}\geq N\lfloor\eta^{-1}\rfloor(16d\log 6+c)-\frac{\Delta}{\eta}\,.

Now, on the whole event, consider the random variable
X\in\mathbb{R}^{N\lfloor\eta^{-1}\rfloor d}
defined by

(D.3.27)

(X_{nd}+1,\dots,X_{(n+1)d})=\frac{\mathsf{U}(x_{n},\omega_{n})}{\sqrt{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}\circ f(x_{n})}}\quad\text{ for }n=0,\dots,N\lfloor\eta^{-1}\rfloor-1\,,

we have that
\prob_{\!#1}\left(\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}>N\right)\leq\prob_{\!#1}\left(\lVert X\rVert^{2}\geq N\lfloor\eta^{-1}\rfloor(16d\log 6+c)-\frac{\Delta}{\eta}\right)
.

Since, for any
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(D.3.28)

\log\ex\left[\exp\left(\left\langle v,\frac{\mathsf{U}(x_{n},\omega_{n})}{\sqrt{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}\circ f(x_{n})}}\right\rangle\right)\nonscript\,\middle|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\right]\leq\frac{\lVert v\rVert^{2}}{2}\,,

the random variable
X
satisfies the assumptions of
Lemma

D.13
with
d\leftarrow N\lfloor\eta^{-1}\rfloor d
.

First, suppose that
t\coloneqq N\lfloor\eta^{-1}\rfloor(16d\log 6+c)-\frac{\Delta}{\eta}
is non-negative. Applying
Lemma

D.13
with this
t
yields that

\displaystyle\prob_{\!#1}\left(\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}>N\right)

\displaystyle\leq\prob_{\!#1}\left(\lVert X\rVert^{2}\geq N\lfloor\eta^{-1}\rfloor(16d\log 6+c)-\frac{\Delta}{\eta}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 6^{N\lfloor\eta^{-1}\rfloor d}\exp\left(-\frac{N\lfloor\eta^{-1}\rfloor(16d\log 6+c)-\frac{\Delta}{\eta}}{8}\right)

(D.3.29)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{N\lfloor\eta^{-1}\rfloor(8d\log 6+c)-\frac{\Delta}{\eta}}{8}\right)\,.

If
t
, defined above, is negative, then in particular
\frac{\Delta}{\eta}\geq N\lfloor\eta^{-1}\rfloor c
so that this bounds still (trivially) holds.

Finally, in particular, for
\eta\leq 1/2
,
\lfloor\eta^{-1}\rfloor\geq(2\eta)^{-1}
so we obtain

(D.3.30)

\prob_{\!#1}\left(\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}>N\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{N(8d\log 6+c)}{16\eta}+\frac{\Delta}{8\eta}\right)

and our proof is complete.
∎

D.4.
Preliminary estimates and lemmas

We will use the following lemma, which corresponds to
Kifer [35, Lem. 5.3]
.

Lemma D.18
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{X}
be a compact set such that
\mathcal{K}\cap\crit f=\emptyset
.
Then there exists
c>0
,
N\geq 1
,
\eta_{0}>0
,
such that, for any
n>N
,
x\in\mathcal{K}
,
\eta\leq\eta_{0}
,

(D.4.1)

\prob_{x}\left(\sigma_{\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{K}}>n\right)=\prob_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{K}}>n\right)\leq\exp\left(-c(n-N)/\eta\right)\,.

Proof.

The proof is exactly the same as the proof
Kifer [35, Lem. 5.3]
, which only uses the
lsc
of
\mathcal{A}_{N}
(
Corollary

C.2
).
∎

The following lemma provides a convenient reformulation of the results of
Lemma

D.17
and
Lemma

D.18
.

Lemma D.19
.

•

There exists
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{X}
a compact set,
\eta_{0}>0
, such that for any
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{X}
compact set such that
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, there exists
\alpha_{0},a,b>0
such that,

(D.4.2)

\forall\eta\leq\eta_{0},\,\alpha\leq\alpha_{0},\,x\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{D}\,,\quad\ex_{x}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}}{\eta}}\right]\leq e^{\frac{a\alpha}{\eta}+b}\,.

•

For any
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{X}
compact such that
\mathcal{K}\cap\crit f=\emptyset
, there exists
\eta_{0},\alpha_{0},a,b>0
such that,

(D.4.3)

\forall\eta\leq\eta_{0},\,\alpha\leq\alpha_{0},\,x\in\mathcal{K}\,,\quad\ex_{x}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha\tau_{\mathcal{K}}}{\eta}}\right]\leq e^{\frac{a\alpha}{\eta}+b}\,.

Proof.

The proofs of both statements are very similar, so we prove only the second one for notational convenience.
Fix
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{X}
compact such that
\mathcal{K}\cap\crit f=\emptyset
.
By
Lemma

D.18
, there exists
c>0
,
N\geq 1
such that, for any
n>N
,
x\in\mathcal{K}
,
\eta\leq\eta_{0}
,

(D.4.4)

\prob_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{K}}>n\right)\leq\exp\left(-c(n-N)/\eta\right)\,.

Let us first bound
\ex_{x}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\alpha(\tau_{\mathcal{K}}-N)}{\eta}\right)\right]
. We have that

\displaystyle\ex_{x}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\alpha(\tau_{\mathcal{K}}-N)}{\eta}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\prob_{x}\left(\exp\left(\frac{\alpha(\tau_{\mathcal{K}}-N)}{\eta}\right)>t\right)\>dt

\displaystyle\leq e^{\frac{\alpha}{\eta}}+{\int_{e^{\frac{\alpha}{\eta}}}^{\infty}\prob_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{K}}>N+\frac{\eta\log t}{\alpha}\right)\>dt}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\frac{\alpha}{\eta}}+{\int_{e^{\frac{\alpha}{\eta}}}^{\infty}\prob_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{K}}>N+\left\lfloor\frac{\eta\log t}{\alpha}\right\rfloor\right)\>dt}

(D.4.5)

\displaystyle\leq e^{\frac{\alpha}{\eta}}+{\int_{e^{\frac{\alpha}{\eta}}}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\frac{c}{\eta}\left\lfloor\frac{\eta\log t}{\alpha}\right\rfloor\right)\>dt}\,,

where we used
Eq.

D.4.4
in the last inequality.

Lower bounding
\lfloor s\rfloor
by
s-1
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\ex_{x}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\alpha(\tau_{\mathcal{K}}-N)}{\eta}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq e^{\frac{\alpha}{\eta}}+{\int_{e^{\frac{\alpha}{\eta}}}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\frac{c}{\eta}\frac{\eta\log t}{\alpha}+\frac{c}{\eta}\right)\>dt}

(D.4.6)

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{\alpha}{\eta}}+{\int_{e^{\frac{\alpha}{\eta}}}^{\infty}e^{\frac{c}{\eta}}t^{-\frac{c}{\alpha}}\>dt}\,.

Performing the change of variable
s\leftarrow e^{-\frac{\alpha}{\eta}}t
, we obtain that

(D.4.7)

\displaystyle\ex_{x}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\alpha(\tau_{\mathcal{K}}-N)}{\eta}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq e^{\frac{\alpha}{\eta}}\left(1+{\int_{1}^{\infty}s^{-\frac{c}{\alpha}}\>ds}\right)\,.

When
\alpha\leq c/2
, we obtain that

(D.4.8)

\ex_{x}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\alpha(\tau_{\mathcal{K}}-N)}{\eta}\right)\right]\leq e^{\frac{\alpha}{\eta}}\left(1+\int_{1}^{\infty}s^{-2}\>ds\right)\,,

and therefore,

(D.4.9)

\ex_{x}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\tau_{\mathcal{K}}}{\eta}\right)\right]\leq e^{\frac{(N+1)\alpha}{\eta}}\left(1+\int_{1}^{\infty}s^{-2}\>ds\right)\,,

which concludes the proof.
∎

The following lemma upper-bounds the probability
of exiting a large neighborhood of the critical points before visiting a smaller one critical points.

Lemma D.20
.

Consider
\crit f\subset\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{X}
with
\mathcal{U}
an open set and
\mathcal{D}
a compact set.
There exists
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{X}
compact set such that
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
,
\Delta>0
,
\eta_{0}>0
such that, for any
\eta\leq\eta_{0}
,
x\in\mathcal{D}
,

(D.4.10)

\prob_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}<\sigma_{\mathcal{U}}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\Delta}{\eta}\right)\,.

Proof.

Define
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\coloneqq\{x\in\mathcal{X}:f(x)\leq\sup_{\mathcal{D}}f+1\}
and let
U_{\infty}
be as in
Lemma

D.6
.

Since
\alpha_{\infty}
is (stricly) increasing as its derivative is (stricly) positive by definition, we have

(D.4.11)

\Delta\coloneqq\alpha_{\infty}\left(\sup_{\mathcal{D}}f+1\right)-\alpha_{\infty}\left(\sup_{\mathcal{D}}f\right)>0\,.

By
Lemma

D.18
applied to
\mathcal{K}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\delta}\setminus\mathcal{U}
, there exists
c>0
,
N_{0}\geq 1
,
\eta_{0}>0
such that for any
n>N_{0}
,
x\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\delta}\setminus\mathcal{U}
,
\eta\leq\eta_{0}
,

(D.4.12)

\prob_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\delta}\setminus\mathcal{U}}>n\right)\leq\exp\left(-c(n-N_{0})/\eta\right)\,.

Defining
N\coloneqq\left\lceil\frac{\Delta}{c}\right\rceil+N_{0}
, which is greater or equal than 1, we obtain that, for any
\eta\leq\eta_{0}
,
x\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\delta}\setminus\mathcal{U}
,

(D.4.13)

\prob_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\delta}\setminus\mathcal{U}}\geq N\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\Delta}{\eta}\right)\,.

Note that this inequality actually holds for any
x\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\delta}
.

We now bound
\prob_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\delta}}>\sigma_{\mathcal{U}}\right)
for
x\in\mathcal{D}
by distinguishing the cases where
\tau_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\delta}}<N
and
\tau_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\delta}}\geq N
. For any
x\in\mathcal{D}
, we have that

\displaystyle\prob_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\delta}}<\sigma_{\mathcal{U}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\prob_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\delta}}<\sigma_{\mathcal{U}},\,\tau_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\delta}}<N\right)+\prob_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\delta}}<\sigma_{\mathcal{U}},\,\tau_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\delta}}\geq N\right)

\displaystyle\leq\prob_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\delta}}<N\right)+\prob_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\delta}\setminus\mathcal{U}}\geq N\right)

(D.4.14)

\displaystyle\leq\prob_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\delta}}<N\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{\Delta}{\eta}\right)

where we used
Eq.

D.4.13
.

We now focus on bounding the first term. For this, we first show that
\tau_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\delta}}<N
implies that

(D.4.15)

\dist_{N}\left(x^{\eta},\Gamma^{\{x\}}_{N}(\Delta/2)\right)>\delta/2\,.

For the sake of contradiction, suppose that this inequality does not hold.
Therefore, there must exist
\xi\in\Gamma^{\{x\}}_{N}(\Delta/4)
such that
\dist_{N}\left(x^{\eta},\xi\right)<\delta
. In particular, there is some
n<N
such that
\xi_{n}\notin\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, so that, by
Definition

3
,

(D.4.16)

\mathcal{A}_{N}(\xi)\geq\mathcal{A}_{n+1}(\xi)\geq B(\xi_{0},\xi_{n})\,.

By
Lemma

D.6
, we have that

\displaystyle B(\xi_{0},\xi_{n})

\displaystyle\geq{U_{\infty}(\xi_{n})-U_{\infty}(\xi_{0})}

(D.4.17)

\displaystyle\geq{\alpha_{\infty}\left(\inf_{\mathcal{X}\setminus{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}}f\right)-\alpha_{\infty}\left(\sup_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}f\right)}\,,

since
\alpha_{\infty}
is increasing. By construction of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, we have further that

(D.4.18)

B(\xi_{0},\xi_{n})\geq{\alpha_{\infty}\left(\sup_{\mathcal{D}}f+1\right)-\alpha_{\infty}\left(\sup_{\mathcal{D}}f)\right)}=\Delta\,,

so that
\mathcal{A}_{N}(\xi)\geq\Delta
, which is a contradiction with
\xi\in\Gamma^{\{x\}}_{N}(\Delta/2)
.

Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle\prob\left(\tau_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\delta}}<N\right)

\displaystyle\leq\prob\left(\dist_{N}\left(x^{\eta},\Gamma^{\{x\}}_{N}(\Delta/2)\right)>\delta/2\right)

(D.4.19)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\Delta}{4\eta}\right)

where we invoked
Corollary

C.2
with
\delta\leftarrow\delta/2
,
s\leftarrow\Delta/2
,
\varepsilon\leftarrow\Delta/4
.
∎

The following lemma is key to our analysis: it shows that
SGD
spends most of its time near its critical points.

Lemma D.21
.

Consider
\crit f\subset\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{X}
with
\mathcal{U}
an open set and
\mathcal{D}
a compact set.
Then, there is some
\eta_{0},\alpha_{0},a,b>0
such that,

(D.4.20)

\forall\eta\leq\eta_{0},\,\alpha\leq\alpha_{0},\,x\in\mathcal{D}\,,\quad\ex_{x}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha\sigma_{\mathcal{U}}}{\eta}}\right]\leq e^{\frac{a\alpha}{\eta}+b}\,.

Proof.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
.
Without loss of generality, assume that
\mathcal{D}
is large enough to include the compact set given by the first item of
Lemma

D.19
(note that the guarantee of the first item of
Lemma

D.19
still holds even if
\mathcal{D}
is larger).

Apply
Lemma

D.20
with
\mathcal{U}\leftarrow\mathcal{U}
,
\mathcal{D}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}
and denote by
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
the obtained compact and
\eta_{0},\Delta>0
such that, for every
\eta\leq\eta_{0}
,
x\in\mathcal{D}
,

(D.4.21)

\prob_{x}\left(\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}}<\sigma_{\mathcal{U}}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\Delta}{\eta}\right)\,.

Define
r\coloneqq\sup_{x\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}}\lVert x\rVert
and
R=e^{8M}(1+r)
.
Assuming that
\eta\leq 1
, by
Lemma

B.2
, for any
x\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
, the next two iterates of
(x^{\eta}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
satisfies
\lVert x^{\eta}_{1}\rVert\leq R
and
\lVert x^{\eta}_{2}\rVert\leq R
.
Define
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\coloneqq\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,R)
.

We invoke both items of
Lemma

D.19
with
\mathcal{K}\leftarrow\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}
and denote by
c>0
a constant that satisfies the bounds of both items. In the rest of the proof, we consider
\eta
and
\alpha
smaller than the bounds given by this lemma.

Our goal is to bound, for any
N\geq 0
, the quantity,

(D.4.22)

s_{N}(\alpha,\eta)\coloneqq\sup_{x\in\mathcal{D}}\ex_{x}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\sigma_{\mathcal{U}}^{N}}{\eta}\right)\right]\,,\quad\text{where}\quad\sigma_{\mathcal{U}}^{N}\coloneqq\min(N,\sigma_{\mathcal{U}})\,.

Note that, by construction,
s_{N}(\alpha,\eta)
is finite.

Take
x\in\mathcal{D}
.
In particular,
Lemma

D.19
implies that
\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}<+\infty
almost surely for all
x\in\mathcal{D}
.

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\sigma_{\mathcal{U}}^{N}}{\eta}\right)

\displaystyle=\one\{x^{\eta}_{\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}\in\mathcal{U}\}\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}{\eta}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\one\{x^{\eta}_{\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}\notin\mathcal{D}_{\delta}\}\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}{\eta}\right)\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\left(\sigma_{\mathcal{U}}^{N}-\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}\right)}{\eta}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\one\{x^{\eta}_{\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}\in\mathcal{U}\}\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}{\eta}\right)

(D.4.23)

\displaystyle\qquad+\one\{x^{\eta}_{\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}\notin\mathcal{D}_{\delta}\}\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}{\eta}\right)\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\min\left(\sigma_{\mathcal{U}}-\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}},N\right)}{\eta}\right)

so that we can apply the strong Markov property to the Markov chain
(x^{\eta}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
with stopping time
\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}
to obtain that

\displaystyle\ex_{x}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\sigma_{\mathcal{U}}^{N}}{\eta}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\ex_{x}\bigg[\one\{x^{\eta}_{\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}\in\mathcal{U}\}\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}{\eta}\right)

(D.4.24)

\displaystyle\qquad+\one\{x^{\eta}_{\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}\notin\mathcal{D}_{\delta}\}\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}{\eta}\right)\ex_{x^{\eta}_{\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\sigma_{\mathcal{U}}^{N}}{\eta}\right)\right]\bigg]

We now bound

(D.4.25)

\one\{x^{\eta}_{\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}\notin\mathcal{D}_{\delta}\}\ex_{x^{\eta}_{\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\sigma_{\mathcal{U}}^{N}}{\eta}\right)\right]=\ex_{x^{\eta}_{\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}}\left[\one\{x^{\eta}_{0}\notin\mathcal{D}_{\delta}\}\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\sigma_{\mathcal{U}}^{N}}{\eta}\right)\right]

Since
x
is in
\mathcal{D}
, by definition,
\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}
is at least equal to 1 and
x^{\eta}_{\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}-1}
is still in
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
. By definition of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
,
x^{\eta}_{\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}
must still be in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.
Therefore, the guarantee of
Lemma

D.19
applies and, since in particular it implies that
\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}
is finite almost surely when the chain is started at
x^{\eta}_{\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}
, we can apply the strong Markov property to obtain that

\displaystyle\ex_{x^{\eta}_{\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}}\left[\one\{x_{0}\notin\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\}\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\sigma_{\mathcal{U}}^{N}}{\eta}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\ex_{x^{\eta}_{\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}}\left[\one\{x_{0}\notin\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\}\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}}{\eta}\right)\ex_{x^{\eta}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}}}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\sigma_{\mathcal{U}}^{N}}{\eta}\right)\right]\right]

(D.4.26)

\displaystyle\leq s_{N}(\alpha,\eta)\ex_{x^{\eta}_{\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}}\left[\one\{x_{0}\notin\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\}\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}}{\eta}\right)\right]

where, for the second inequality, we used the definition of
s_{N}(\alpha,\eta)
and the fact that
x_{\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}}
is in
\mathcal{D}
.

Now, to bound the remaining expectation, note that
x_{0}
does not belong to
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
and,
a fortiori
, does not belong to
\mathcal{D}
. Therefore,
\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}
depends only on
(x^{\eta}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and the (weak) Markov property implies that

\displaystyle\ex_{x^{\eta}_{\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}}\left[\one\{x_{0}\notin\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\}\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}}{\eta}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\ex_{x^{\eta}_{\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}}\left[\one\{x_{0}\notin\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\}\ex_{x_{1}}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\alpha(1+\sigma_{\mathcal{D}})}{\eta}\right)\right]\right]

(D.4.27)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\frac{\alpha(1+c)}{\eta}\right)\ex_{x^{\eta}_{\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}}\left[\one\{x_{0}\notin\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\}\right]\,,

by
Lemma

D.19
, since the
x^{\eta}_{\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}-1}
is still in
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
so that
x_{1}
of the chain started at
x^{\eta}_{\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}
is still in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.

Lemma

D.20
then implies that,

(D.4.28)

\displaystyle\ex_{x^{\eta}_{\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}}\left[\one\{x_{0}\notin\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\}\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\sigma_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}}{\eta}\right)\right]\leq\exp\left(\frac{\alpha(1+a)-\Delta}{\eta}+b\right)

Combining these bounds with
Eq.

D.4.24
then gives

\displaystyle\ex_{x}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\sigma_{\mathcal{U}}^{N}}{\eta}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+s_{N}(\alpha,\eta)\exp\left(\frac{\alpha(1+a)-\Delta}{\eta}+b\right)\right)\ex_{x}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\tau_{\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\mathcal{U}}}{\eta}\right)\right]

(D.4.29)

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+s_{N}(\alpha,\eta)\exp\left(\frac{\alpha(1+a)-\Delta}{\eta}+b\right)\right)\exp\left(\frac{a\alpha}{\eta}+b\right)\,,

by
Lemma

D.19
.
Since this inequality is valid for any
x\in\mathcal{D}
, we have shown that

(D.4.30)

s_{N}(\alpha,\eta)\leq\left(e^{\frac{a\alpha}{\eta}+b}+s_{N}(\alpha,\eta)e^{\frac{\alpha(1+2a)-\Delta}{\eta}+b}\right)\,.

For
\alpha\leq\Delta/(2(1+2a))
and
\eta
small enough,

(D.4.31)

e^{\frac{\alpha(1+2a)-\Delta}{\eta}+b}\leq e^{-\frac{\Delta}{2\eta}+b}\leq{\frac{1}{2}}\,,

and we obtain that

(D.4.32)

s_{N}(\alpha,\eta)\leq 2e^{\frac{a\alpha}{\eta}+b}\,.

Taking
N\to+\infty
and using Fatou’s lemma yields that

(D.4.33)

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{D}}\ex_{x}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\sigma_{\mathcal{U}}}{\eta}\right)\right]\leq 2e^{\frac{a\alpha}{\eta}+b}\,,

which concludes the proof.
∎

D.5.
Estimates of the invariant measure

Define, for sets
A,B\subset\mathcal{X}
,

(D.5.1)

B(A,B)\coloneqq\inf\{B(x,z):x\in A,z\in B\}\,.

Recall that for any
i,j
, we define

(D.5.2)

B_{i,j}=B(\mathcal{K}_{i},\mathcal{K}_{j})=\inf\{B(x,z):x\in\mathcal{K}_{i},z\in\mathcal{K}_{j}\}\,.

Recall that, to avoid degenerate cases, we assume that
Assumption

4
holds, that is,

(D.5.3)

B_{ij}<\infty\quad\text{for all $i,j=1,\dotsc,K$}.

With
Lemma

D.12
, this assumption is satisfied in particular if the following condition holds: for any
i,j=1,\dotsc,K
, there exists a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
path
\gamma
joining
\mathcal{K}_{i}
and
\mathcal{K}_{j}
such that, for all
t
,
\nabla f(\gamma_{t})
belongs to the interior of the closed convex hull of the support of the noise
\mathsf{U}(\gamma_{t},\omega)
:

(D.5.4)

\nabla f(\gamma_{t})\in\intr\clconv\supp\mathsf{U}(\gamma_{t},\omega)\,,

This means there is a sufficient level of noise for the probability of going from
\mathcal{K}_{i}
to
\mathcal{K}_{j}
with at least one path to be non-zero (though it can be vanishingly small).
Note that it does not constrain the nature of the noise itself — which can be discrete, continuous or else —, only the support of its distribution.

Moreover, if
Assumption

4
did not hold, the same analysis as in this section could still be carried out. We would consider the components of the graph
\mathcal{G}
connected by edges with finite weights proceed with the proof on each of them, and obtain the same results on each of these components. To keep the complexity of the proof reasonable, we will not consider this case here.

We adapt to our context
Kifer [35, Lem. 5.4]
and simplify it using ideas from
Freidlin & Wentzell [20, Chap. 6]
.

Definition 6

(
Freidlin & Wentzell [20, Chap. 6,§2]
)
.

For
i,j
,

(D.5.5)

\widetilde{B}_{i,j}\coloneqq\inf\left\{\mathcal{A}_{N}(\xi):N\geq 1,\xi\in\mathcal{X}^{N},\,\xi_{0}\in\mathcal{K}_{i},\,\xi_{N-1}\in\mathcal{K}_{j},\,\xi_{n}\notin\bigcup_{l\neq i,j}\mathcal{K}_{l}\text{ for }n=1,\dots,N-2\right\}\,.

We now defined an important object: the law of the (accelerated) iterated at the first time they reach some set
\mathcal{V}
(typically a neighborhood of the critical set), following e.g.,
Douc et al. [14, Chap. 3.4]
.

Definition 7

(
Kifer [35, Prop. 5.3]
)
.

For
\mathcal{V}
open set, with hitting time (of the accelerated sequence)

\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\coloneqq\inf\{n\geq 1:x^{\eta}_{n}\in\mathcal{V}\}\,,
(as in
Definition

5
), denote the law of
x^{\eta}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}}
started at
x
by
\probalt_{\mathcal{V}}(x,\cdot)
and the corresponding
N
-step transition probability by
\probalt_{\mathcal{V}}^{N}(x,\cdot)
.

In words,
\probalt_{\mathcal{V}}(x,\cdot)
is the distributions of the
x^{\eta}
started at
x
at the first time they reach
\mathcal{V}
. Typically,
\probalt_{\mathcal{V}}(x,\mathcal{U})
is the probability that
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{V}
is reached first among
\mathcal{V}
. Then,
\probalt_{\mathcal{V}}^{N}(x,\cdot)
is the distribution of the
x^{\eta}
started at
x
at the
N
-th time they reach
\mathcal{V}
.

We first give estimates of the transition probabilities using the
\widetilde{B}_{i,j}
. We will then translate them to
B_{i,j}
.

Lemma D.22
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, for any small enough neighborhoods
\mathcal{V}_{i}
of
\mathcal{K}_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,K
, there is some
\eta_{0}>0
such that for all
i,j
,
x\in\mathcal{V}_{i}
,
0<\eta<\eta_{0}
,

(D.5.6)

\probalt_{\mathcal{V}}(x,\mathcal{V}_{j})\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\widetilde{B}_{i,j}}{\eta}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,.

where we defined
\mathcal{V}\coloneqq\bigcup_{i=1}^{K}\mathcal{V}_{i}
.

Proof.

Assume that, without loss of generality,
\varepsilon
is small enough so that
Lemma

D.8
with
r\leftarrow\varepsilon
can be applied to every
\mathcal{K}_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,K
. Denote by
\mathcal{W}_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,K
the corresponding neighborhoods of
\mathcal{K}_{i}
.

Since these
\mathcal{W}_{i}
’s are neighborhoods of the
\mathcal{K}_{i}
’s,
there exists
\delta>0
such that
\mathcal{U}_{2\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})\subset\mathcal{W}_{i}
for all
i=1,\dots,K
.
Require then that
\mathcal{V}_{i}
be contained in
\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
so that

\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{V}_{i})\subset\mathcal{W}_{i}
for all
i=1,\dots,K
.
Moreover, assume that
\delta>0
is small enough so that the neighborhoods
\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
,
i=1,\dots,K
are pairwise disjoint.

Define
0<\delta^{\prime}\leq\delta
such that
\mathcal{U}_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
is contained in
\mathcal{V}_{i}
for all
i=1,\dots,K
.

Fix
i,j\in I
and consider
\xi\in(\mathcal{X})^{N}
such that
\xi_{0}\in\mathcal{U}_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathcal{V}_{i})\subset\mathcal{W}_{i}
,
\xi_{N-1}\in\mathcal{U}_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathcal{V}_{j})\subset\mathcal{W}_{j}
and
\xi_{n}\in\mathcal{U}_{\delta^{\prime}}\left(\mathcal{X}\setminus\bigcup_{l\neq i,j}\mathcal{V}_{l}\right)
for all
n=1,\dots,N-2
.
By the choice of
\delta^{\prime}
,
\xi_{n}
cannot be in
\bigcup_{l\neq i,j}\mathcal{K}_{l}
for any
n=1,\dots,N-2
.

By definition of
\mathcal{W}_{i}
and
\mathcal{W}_{j}
, there are
x\in\mathcal{K}_{i}
,
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}_{j}
such that
\rho(x,\xi_{0})<\varepsilon
,
\rho(\xi_{N-1},x^{\prime})<\varepsilon
. Therefore, the path
\zeta\in(\mathcal{X})^{N+2}
defined as
\zeta=(x,\xi_{0},\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{N-1},x^{\prime})
satisfies

(D.5.7)

\mathcal{A}_{N}(\xi)\geq\mathcal{A}_{N+2}(\zeta)-2\varepsilon\,.

and, by definition of
\widetilde{B}_{i,j}
, we thus obtain,

(D.5.8)

\mathcal{A}_{N}(\xi)\geq\mathcal{A}_{N+2}(\zeta)-2\varepsilon\geq\widetilde{B}_{i,j}-2\varepsilon\,.

Fix
x\in\mathcal{V}_{i}
. Let us now bound the probability

(D.5.9)

\probalt_{\mathcal{V}}(x,\mathcal{V}_{j})=\prob_{x}\left(x^{\eta}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}}\in\mathcal{V}_{i}\right)\,.

We have, for any
N\geq 0
,

(D.5.10)

\prob_{x}\left(x^{\eta}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}}\in\mathcal{V}_{i}\right)\leq\prob_{x}\left(x^{\eta}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}}\in\mathcal{V}_{i},\,\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}<N\right)+\prob_{x}\left(\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\geq N\right)\,.

We first bound the second probability using
Lemma

D.21
applied to
\mathcal{U}\leftarrow\mathcal{V}_{i}
.
Take
N
such that
\alpha_{0}(a-N)+\eta_{0}b\leq-\widetilde{B}_{i,j}
.
Then, by Markov’s inequality and
Lemma

D.21
, it holds that for all
\eta\leq\eta_{0}

\displaystyle\prob_{x}\left(\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\geq N\right)

\displaystyle\leq\prob_{x}\left(\exp\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}}{\eta}\right)\geq\exp\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}N}{\eta}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}(a-N)}{\eta}+b\right)

(D.5.11)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\frac{-\widetilde{B}_{i,j}}{\eta}\right)\,.

We now bound the term
\prob_{x}\left(x^{\eta}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}}\in\mathcal{V}_{j},\,\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}<N\right)
for this choice of
N
.

For this, we show that
x^{\eta}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}}\in\mathcal{V}_{j}
with
