\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}<N
implies that

(D.5.12)

\dist_{N}\left(x^{\eta},\Gamma_{N}^{\{x\}}\left(\widetilde{B}_{i,j}-3\varepsilon\right)\right)>{\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2}}\,.

Indeed, on the event
x^{\eta}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}}\in\mathcal{V}_{j}
with
\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}<N
, there is some
N^{\prime}\leq N
such that
\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}=N^{\prime}-1
.
If
\dist_{N}\left(x^{\eta},\Gamma_{N}^{\{x\}}\left(\widetilde{B}_{i,j}-3\varepsilon\right)\right)>{\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2}}
did not hold, this would mean that there exists
\xi\in(\mathcal{X})^{N^{\prime}}
such that
\dist_{N^{\prime}}(x^{\eta},\xi)<\delta^{\prime}
,
\xi_{0}=x
and,

\mathcal{A}_{N^{\prime}}(\xi)\leq\widetilde{B}_{i,j}-3\varepsilon
. In particular,
\xi
would also satisfy

\xi_{N^{\prime}-1}\in\mathcal{U}_{\delta^{\prime}}(\mathcal{V}_{j})
,
\xi_{n}\in\mathcal{U}_{\delta^{\prime}}(\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{V})
for all
n=1,\dots,N^{\prime}-2
. This would be in direct contradiction of
Eq.

D.5.8
.

Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle\prob_{x}\left(x^{\eta}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}}\in\mathcal{V}_{j},\,\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}<N\right)

\displaystyle\leq\prob_{x}\left(\dist_{N}\left(x^{\eta},\Gamma_{N}^{\{x\}}\left(\widetilde{B}_{i,j}-3\varepsilon\right)\right)>{\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2}}\right)

(D.5.13)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\widetilde{B}_{i,j}-4\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,,

by
Corollary

C.2
.

Combining this bound with
Eq.

D.5.11
yields

(D.5.14)

\prob_{x}(x^{\eta}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}}\in\mathcal{V}_{j})\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\widetilde{B}_{i,j}-4\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)+\exp\left(\frac{-\widetilde{B}_{i,j}}{\eta}\right)\,,

which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma D.23
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
,for any neighborhoods
\mathcal{V}_{i}
of
\mathcal{K}_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,K
small enough, there exists
N\geq 0
,
\eta_{0}>0
such that for all
i,j
,
x\in\mathcal{V}_{i}
,
0<\eta<\eta_{0}
,

(D.5.15)

\probalt_{\mathcal{V}}^{N}(x,\mathcal{V}_{j})\geq\exp\left(-\frac{\widetilde{B}_{i,j}}{\eta}-\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,.

Proof.

For any
i,j
, there exists
N_{i,j}\geq 1
,
\xi^{i,j}\in(\mathcal{X})^{N_{i,j}}
such that
\xi^{i,j}_{0}\in\mathcal{K}_{i}
,
\xi^{i,j}_{N_{i,j}-1}\in\mathcal{K}_{j}
,
\xi^{i,j}_{n}\notin\bigcup_{l\neq i,j}\mathcal{K}_{l}
for all
n=1,\dots,N_{i,j}-2
and
\mathcal{A}_{N_{i,j}}(\xi^{i,j})\leq\widetilde{B}_{i,j}+\varepsilon
. Define
\delta_{i,j}\coloneqq\min\left\{d(\xi^{i,j}_{n},\bigcup_{l\neq i,j}\mathcal{K}_{l}):n=1,\dots,N_{i,j}-2\right\}
and
\delta\coloneqq\min_{i,j\in I}\delta_{i,j}
. By construction, it holds that
\delta>0
.

Require that
\mathcal{V}_{i}
be contained in
\mathcal{W}_{i}\cap\mathcal{U}_{\delta/2}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
for all
i=1,\dots,K
.
Now, given such
\mathcal{V}_{i}
neighborhoods of
\mathcal{K}_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,K
, there exists
0<\delta^{\prime}\leq\delta/2
such that
\mathcal{U}_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
is contained in
\mathcal{V}_{i}
for all
i=1,\dots,K
.

Apply
Lemma

D.9
to
\mathcal{K}_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,K
with
\varepsilon\leftarrow\min(\varepsilon,\delta^{\prime}/2)
and denote by
N_{i}
the bound on the length of paths obtained. Define

(D.5.16)

N\coloneqq\max_{i\in I}N_{i}+1\,.

Fix
i,j\in I
and
x\in\mathcal{V}_{i}
. Since
\mathcal{V}_{i}\subset\mathcal{W}_{i}
, there exists
z\in\mathcal{K}_{i}
such that
\rho(x,z)<\varepsilon
. Moreover, note that
\rho(z,z)=0
since
z
is a critical point of
f
.

By
Lemma

D.9
, there exists
n\leq N
,
\xi\in(\mathcal{X})^{n}
such that
\xi_{0}=z
,
\xi_{N-1}=\xi^{i,j}_{0}
,
\xi_{k}\in\mathcal{U}_{\delta^{\prime}/2}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
for all
k=1,\dots,n-2
and
\mathcal{A}_{n}(\xi)<\varepsilon
.

Considering the concatenation

(D.5.17)

\zeta\coloneqq\left(x,\underbrace{z,z,\dots,z}_{N-n\text{ times}},\xi_{0},\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n-2},\xi_{n-1},\xi^{i,j}_{1},\dots,\xi^{i,j}_{N_{i,j}-1}\right)

which is a path of length
N+N_{i,j}
made of
x\in\mathcal{V}_{i}
, then exactly
N
points in
\mathcal{U}_{\delta^{\prime}/2}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
then
N_{i,j}-2
in
\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{\delta/2}(\mathcal{V})
and
\xi^{i,j}_{N_{i,j}-1}\in\mathcal{K}_{j}
.
Moreover, by construction,
\mathcal{A}_{N+N_{i,j}}(\zeta)\leq\widetilde{B}_{i,j}+3\varepsilon
.
Therefore, if

(D.5.18)

\dist_{N+N_{i,j}}\left(x^{\eta},\zeta\right)<\delta^{\prime}/2\,,

with
x^{\eta}_{0}=x
, then
x^{\eta}_{1},\dots,x^{\eta}_{N}
are in
\mathcal{U}_{\delta^{\prime}}(\mathcal{K}_{i})\subset\mathcal{V}_{i}
and, since
\delta^{\prime}\leq\delta/2
,
x^{\eta}_{N+1},\dots,x^{\eta}_{N+N_{i,j}-2}
are not in
\mathcal{U}_{\delta/4}(\mathcal{V})
, and therefore not in
\mathcal{V}
. Moreover,
x^{\eta}_{N+N_{i,j}-1}
would be in
\mathcal{U}_{\delta^{\prime}/2}(\mathcal{K}_{j})\subset\mathcal{V}_{j}
.

Thus, all the paths
x^{\eta}
satisfying (
D.5.18
) with
x^{\eta}_{0}=x
are started at
x
and their
N
-th point that fall into
\mathcal{V}
belongs to
\mathcal{V}_{j}
.

Therefore, using the definition of
\probalt_{\mathcal{V}}^{N}
, we have that

\displaystyle\probalt_{\mathcal{V}}^{N}(x,\mathcal{V}_{j})

\displaystyle\geq\prob_{x}\left(\dist_{N+N_{i,j}}\left(x^{\eta},\zeta\right)<\delta^{\prime}/2\right)

(D.5.19)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(-\frac{\widetilde{B}_{i,j}+4\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,,

by
Corollary

C.2
.
∎

Lemma D.24
.

For any
i,j\in I
,

\displaystyle B_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\sum_{l=0}^{n-2}\widetilde{B}_{i_{l},i_{l+1}}:i_{0}=i,\,i_{n-1}=j,\,i_{l}\in I\text{ for }l=1,\dots,n-2\,,n\geq 1\right\}

(D.5.20)

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\sum_{l=0}^{K-2}\widetilde{B}_{i_{l},i_{l+1}}:i_{0}=i,\,i_{K-1}=j,\,i_{l}\in I\text{ for }l=1,\dots,K-2\right\}\,.

Proof.

It suffices to show that

(D.5.21)

B_{i,j}=\min\left\{\sum_{l=0}^{n-2}\widetilde{B}_{i_{l},i_{l+1}}:i_{0}=i,\,i_{n-1}=j,\,i_{l}\in I\text{ for }l=1,\dots,n-2\,,n\geq 1\right\}\,.

The statement of the lemma then follows from the fact that
\widetilde{B}_{l,l}=0
for all
l\in I
and that shortest paths on graphs can be chosen not to visit the same node twice.

For the inequality
(\geq)
, notice that any path between
\mathcal{K}_{i}
and
\mathcal{K}_{j}
can be decomposed into a concatenation of paths between
\mathcal{K}_{i}
and
\mathcal{K}_{i_{1}}
,
\mathcal{K}_{i_{1}}
and
\mathcal{K}_{i_{2}}
, …,
\mathcal{K}_{i_{n-1}}
and
\mathcal{K}_{j}
for some
i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1}\in I
that do not enter any other equivalence class in between. Therefore, the inequality
(\geq)
follows from the definition of
B_{i,j}
and
\widetilde{B}_{i_{l},i_{l+1}}
.

We now focus on
(\leq)
.

Fix
\varepsilon
.
Take
n\geq 1
,
i_{0}=i
,
i_{n-1}=j
,
i_{l}\in I
for
l=1,\dots,n-2
.
There are paths
\xi^{0},\dots,\xi^{n-2}
of lengths
N_{0},\dots,N_{n-2}
such that
\xi^{l}_{0}\in\mathcal{K}_{i_{l}}
,
\xi^{l}_{N_{l}-1}\in\mathcal{K}_{i_{l+1}}
,
\mathcal{A}_{N_{l}}(\xi^{l})\leq\widetilde{B}_{i_{l},i_{l+1}}+\varepsilon/n
for
l=0,\dots,n-2
.

By
Lemma

D.9
, for all
l=0,\dots,n-2
,
\xi^{l}_{N_{l}-1}
and
\xi^{l+1}_{0}
can be connected by path of cost at most
\varepsilon/n
. Therefore concatenating all these paths yield
\zeta
of length
N
with
\zeta_{0}\in\mathcal{K}_{i}
,
\zeta_{N-1}\in\mathcal{K}_{j}
and
\mathcal{A}_{N}(\zeta)\leq\sum_{l=0}^{n-2}\widetilde{B}_{i_{l},i_{l+1}}+2\varepsilon
. Since
\mathcal{A}_{N}(\zeta)\geq B_{i,j}
, we obtain the desired result.
∎

Notation 1
.

We will write, for non-decreasing
f\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
,

(D.5.22)

a\asymp f(b\pm c)\iff f(b-c)\leq a\leq f(b+c)\,.

Proposition D.2
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
and any small enough neighborhoods
\mathcal{V}_{i}
of
\mathcal{K}_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,K
, there exists
N\geq 0
,
\eta_{0}>0
such that for all
i,j
,
x\in\mathcal{V}_{i}
,
0<\eta<\eta_{0}
,

(D.5.23)

\probalt_{\mathcal{V}}^{N}(x,\mathcal{V}_{j})\asymp\exp\left(-\frac{B_{i,j}}{\eta}\pm\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,.

Proof.

Let us first start with
(\geq)
.

Let
N
satisfy the conditions of
Lemma

D.23
and define
N^{\prime}\coloneqq(K-1)N
.
For any
i,j\in I
, by
Lemma

D.24
, there exist
i_{0}=i
,
i_{K-1}=j
,
i_{l}\in I
for
l=1,\dots,K-2
such that

(D.5.24)

B_{i,j}=\sum_{l=0}^{K-2}\widetilde{B}_{i_{l},i_{l+1}}\,.

Therefore, we have that the probability of reaching
\mathcal{V}_{j}
from
x\in\mathcal{V}_{i}
in
N^{\prime}=(K-1)N
steps is greater than the probability of reaching sequentially the
\mathcal{V}_{i_{l+1}}
from any point
x\in\mathcal{V}_{i_{l}}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\inf_{x\in\mathcal{V}_{i}}\probalt_{\mathcal{V}}^{N^{\prime}}(x,\mathcal{V}_{j})

\displaystyle\geq\prod_{l=0}^{K-2}\inf_{x\in\mathcal{V}_{i_{l}}}\probalt_{\mathcal{V}}^{N}(x,\mathcal{V}_{i_{l+1}})

(D.5.25)

\displaystyle\geq\prod_{l=0}^{K-2}\exp\left(-\frac{\widetilde{B}_{i_{l},i_{l+1}}}{\eta}-\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)=\exp\left(-\frac{B_{i,j}}{\eta}-\frac{(K-1)\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,

where we used
Lemma

D.23
to get the second inequality.

Now for the reverse inequality
(\leq)
. Take
i,j\in I
and denote
i_{0}=i
,
i_{N^{\prime}-1}=j
. By
Lemma

D.22
, we have that

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathcal{V}_{i}}\probalt_{\mathcal{V}}^{N^{\prime}}(x,\mathcal{V}_{j})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{K-2}\in I}\prod_{l=0}^{K-2}\sup_{x\in\mathcal{V}_{i_{l}}}\probalt_{\mathcal{V}}^{N}(x,\mathcal{V}_{i_{l+1}})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{K-2}\in I}\prod_{l=0}^{K-2}\exp\left(-\frac{\widetilde{B}_{i_{l},i_{l+1}}}{\eta}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)

(D.5.26)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{K-2}\in I}\exp\left(-\frac{\sum_{l=0}^{N^{\prime}}\widetilde{B}_{i_{l},i_{l+1}}}{\eta}+\frac{(K-1)\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,.

Using
Lemma

D.24
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathcal{V}_{i}}\probalt_{\mathcal{V}}^{N^{\prime}}(x,\mathcal{V}_{j})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{K-2}\in I}\exp\left(-\frac{B_{i,j}}{\eta}+\frac{(K-1)\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)

(D.5.27)

\displaystyle={K}^{K-2}\exp\left(-\frac{B_{i,j}}{\eta}+\frac{(K-1)\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)

which concludes the proof.
∎

Let us now give our first estimates on the invariant measure.

Definition 8

(
[
20
, Chap. 6,§4]
)
.

Define, for every
i\in I
,

(D.5.28)

E_{i}=E(\mathcal{K}_{i})\coloneqq\min_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{i}}\sum_{(j\to l)\in T}B_{j,l}\,,

where
\mathcal{T}_{i}
denotes the set of trees rooted at
i
in the complete graph on
I
.

Proposition D.3
.

For any
\mu_{\mathcal{V}}
invariant probability measure for
\probalt_{\mathcal{V}}
, the induced chain on
\mathcal{V}
, in the setting of
Proposition

D.2
, for any
i\in I
,

(D.5.29)

\mu_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{V}_{i})\asymp\exp\left(-\frac{E(\mathcal{K}_{i})-\min_{j\in I}E(\mathcal{K}_{j})}{\eta}\pm\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,.

Proof.

If
\mu_{\mathcal{V}}
is an invariant measure of
\probalt_{\mathcal{V}}
, then it is an invariant measure of
\probalt_{\mathcal{V}}^{N}
for any
N
given by
Proposition

D.2
.

Freidlin & Wentzell [20, Chap .6, Lem. 3.1-3.2]
combined with
Proposition

D.2
then give

\displaystyle\exp(-(2\card I+2)\varepsilon/\eta)\frac{\exp(-E(\mathcal{K}_{i})/\eta)}{\sum_{j\in I}\exp(-E(\mathcal{K}_{j})/\eta)}

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{V}_{i})

(D.5.30)

\displaystyle\leq\exp((2\card I+2)\varepsilon/\eta)\frac{\exp(-E(\mathcal{K}_{i})/\eta)}{\sum_{j\in I}\exp(-E(\mathcal{K}_{j})/\eta)}\,.

For
\eta
small enough, it holds that

(D.5.31)

\sum_{j\in I}\exp(-E(\mathcal{K}_{j})/\eta)\asymp\exp\left(-\min_{j\in I}E(\mathcal{K}_{j})/\eta\pm\varepsilon/\eta\right)\,,

which concludes the proof.
∎

We now state a result that links invariant measures of
(x^{\eta}_{n})_{n}
and
\probalt_{\mathcal{V}}
.
It is a consequence of
Douc et al. [14, Thm. 3.6.5]
.

Lemma D.25
.

There is
\eta_{0}>0
such that, for
0<\eta\leq\eta_{0}
,
if
(x^{\eta}_{n})_{n}
has an invariant probability measure
\mu_{\infty}
,
then, for any
\mathcal{V}
measurable neighborhood of
\crit f
,
we have that
\mu_{\infty}(\mathcal{V})>0
and
\mu_{\mathcal{V}}
, the restriction of
{\mu_{\infty}}/{\mu_{\infty}(\mathcal{V})}
to
\mathcal{V}
is an invariant measure for the induced chain on
\mathcal{V}
and, for any measurable set
E\subset\mathcal{X}
,

(D.5.32)

\frac{\mu_{\infty}(E)}{\mu_{\infty}(\mathcal{V})}=\int_{\mathcal{V}}\>d\mu_{\mathcal{V}}(x)\ex_{x}\left[\sum_{n=0}^{\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}-1}\one\{x^{\eta}_{n}\in E\}\right]\,.

Proof.

We invoke the first item of
Lemma

D.19
: there exists
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{X}
a compact set,
\eta_{0}>0
, such that for any
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{X}
compact set such that
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, there exists
\alpha_{0}>0
such that,

(D.5.33)

\forall\eta\leq\eta_{0},\,x\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\,,\quad\ex_{x}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha_{0}\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}}{\eta}}\right]<+\infty\,.

Without loss of generality, at the potential expense of expanding
\mathcal{D}
, assume that
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{D}
.

By applying
Lemma

D.21
to
\mathcal{U}\leftarrow\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{D}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}
, we get that there is some
\eta_{0},\alpha_{0},>0
such that, for any
\eta\leq\eta_{0}
,
x\in\mathcal{D}
,

(D.5.34)

\ex_{x}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}}{\eta}}\right]<+\infty\,.

In particular, we have that
\prob_{x}(\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}<\infty)=1
for any
x\in\mathcal{D}
, and
a fortiori
for any
x\in\mathcal{V}
.

Let us now show that, for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
\prob_{x}(\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}<\infty)=1
.
Fix
x\in\mathcal{X}
. By choosing
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
large enough to contain both
\mathcal{D}
and
x
,

Eq.

D.5.33
implies that, with
x_{0}=x
,
\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}<\infty
almost surely.
Therefore, by the strong Markov property, it holds that,

(D.5.35)

\prob_{x}(\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}<\infty)\geq\inf_{z\in\mathcal{D}}\prob_{z}(\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}<\infty)=1\,.

Therefore the assumptions of
Douc et al. [14, Thm. 3.6.5]
are satisfied as well as its first item which yields the result.
∎

We reach the next proposition, which is the first part of our main result. It is an adaptation of
Freidlin & Wentzell [20, Thm. 4.1]
to the discrete time setting.

Proposition D.4
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, for any
\mathcal{V}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{V}_{K}
measurable neighborhoods of
\mathcal{K}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{K}_{K}
small enough, there exists
\eta_{0}>0
such that
for any
0<\eta<\eta_{0}
,

\mu_{\infty}
invariant probability measure for
(x^{\eta}_{n})_{n}
,
for any
i\in I
,

(D.5.36)

\mu_{\infty}(\mathcal{V}_{i})\asymp\exp\left(-\frac{E(\mathcal{K}_{i})-\min_{j\in I}E(\mathcal{K}_{j})}{\eta}\pm\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,.

Proof.

Let us first provide estimates for the unnormalized measure defined by, for any measurable set
E
,

(D.5.37)

\widetilde{\mu}(E)\coloneqq\int_{E}\>d\mu_{\mathcal{V}}(x)\ex_{x}\left[\sum_{n=0}^{\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}-1}\one\{x^{\eta}_{n}\in E\}\right]\,.

By definition of
\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}
, in the sequence of points
x^{\eta}_{0},\dots,x^{\eta}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}-1}
, only
x^{\eta}_{0}
can be in
\mathcal{V}
.
Therefore, for any
i,j\in I
,
x\in\mathcal{V}_{j}
,

(D.5.38)

\ex_{x}\left[\sum_{n=0}^{\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}-1}\one\{x^{\eta}_{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{i}\}\right]=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }i=j\,,\\
0&\text{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

Thus, we have that,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}(\mathcal{V}_{i})

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{V}_{i})

(D.5.39)

\displaystyle\asymp\exp\left(-\frac{E(\mathcal{K}_{i})-\min_{j\in I}E(\mathcal{K}_{j})}{\eta}\pm\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,,

by
Proposition

D.3
.
It now remains to estimate the normalization constant
\widetilde{\mu}(\mathcal{X})
.
On the one hand, we have that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}(\mathcal{X})

\displaystyle\geq\max_{i\in I}\widetilde{\mu}(\mathcal{V}_{i})

(D.5.40)

\displaystyle\geq\max_{i\in I}\exp\left(-\frac{E(\mathcal{K}_{i})-\min_{j\in I}E(\mathcal{K}_{j})}{\eta}-\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)=\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,.

On the other hand, by
Lemma

D.21
applied with
\mathcal{U}\leftarrow\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{D}\leftarrow\cl{\mathcal{V}}
(choosing
\mathcal{V}
small enough so that is bounded), the quantity

(D.5.41)

c\coloneqq\sup\{\ex_{x}[\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}]:x\in\mathcal{V},\,0<\eta\leq\eta_{0}\}

is finite.
Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}(\mathcal{X})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\>d\mu_{\mathcal{V}}(x)\ex_{x}\left[\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\right]

(D.5.42)

\displaystyle\leq c\int_{\mathcal{X}}\>d\mu_{\mathcal{V}}(x)=c\,,

which, along with choosing
\eta_{0}
small enough so that
c\leq\exp(\varepsilon/\eta)
, concludes the proof.
∎

We will need the following lemma to prove the second part of our main result.

Lemma D.26
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, for any
\mathcal{V}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{V}_{K}
measurable neighborhoods of
\mathcal{K}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{K}_{K}
small enough,
D
measurable set,
\delta_{D}>0
,
there exists
\eta_{0}>0
such that,
for any
0<\eta<\eta_{0}
,
for any
i\in I
,
x\in\mathcal{V}_{i}
,

(D.5.43)

\prob_{x}\left(\sigma_{D_{-\delta_{D}}}<\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{B(\mathcal{K}_{i},D)-\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,,

where

(D.5.44)

D_{-\delta_{D}}\coloneqq\left\{z\in D:d(z,\mathcal{X}\setminus D)\geq\delta_{D}\right\}\,.

Proof.

The requirement on the
\mathcal{V}_{i}
are the same in the proof of
Lemma

D.22
but we restate them here for completeness.

Assume that, without loss of generality,
\varepsilon
is small enough so that
Lemma

D.8
with
r\leftarrow\varepsilon
can be applied to every
\mathcal{K}_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,K
. Denote by
\mathcal{W}_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,K
the corresponding neighborhoods of
\mathcal{K}_{i}
.
Since these
\mathcal{W}_{i}
’s are neighborhoods of the
\mathcal{K}_{i}
’s, there exists
\delta>0
such that
\mathcal{U}_{2\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})\subset\mathcal{W}_{i}
for all
i=1,\dots,K
.
Require then that
\mathcal{V}_{i}
be contained in
\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
so that

\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\cl\mathcal{V}_{i})\subset\mathcal{W}_{i}
for all
i=1,\dots,K
.
Moreover, assume that
\delta>0
is small enough so that the neighborhoods
\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
,
i=1,\dots,K
are pairwise disjoint and that
\delta\leq\delta_{D}
.
Define
0<\delta^{\prime}\leq\delta
such that
\mathcal{U}_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
is contained in
\mathcal{V}_{i}
for all
i=1,\dots,K
.

Fix
i\in I
and consider
\xi\in(\mathcal{X})^{N}
such that
\xi_{0}\in\mathcal{U}_{\delta}^{\prime}(\cl\mathcal{V}_{i})
,
\xi_{N-1}\in\mathcal{U}_{\delta}^{\prime}(D_{-\delta_{D}})
.
By construction,
\xi_{0}\in\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{V}_{i})\subset\mathcal{W}_{i}
. Therefore, there exists
x\in\mathcal{K}_{i}
such that
\rho(x,\xi_{0})<\varepsilon
.
Moreover, since
\delta^{\prime}\leq\delta\leq\delta_{D}
,
\mathcal{U}_{\delta}^{\prime}(D_{-\delta_{D}})\subset D
so that
\xi_{N-1}\in D
.

Define
\zeta\coloneqq(x,\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{N-1})
which is a path from
\mathcal{K}_{i}
to
D
so that

(D.5.45)

\mathcal{A}_{N}(\xi)\geq\mathcal{A}_{N+1}(\zeta)-\varepsilon\geq B\left(\mathcal{K}_{i},D\right)-\varepsilon\,.

We now follow the same outline as for the proof of
Lemma

D.22
.
Fix
x\in\mathcal{V}_{i}
. For any
N\geq 0
, we have that

(D.5.46)

\displaystyle\prob_{x}\left(\sigma_{D_{-\delta_{D}}}<\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\prob_{x}\left(\sigma_{D_{-\delta_{D}}}<\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\,,\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}<N\right)+\prob_{x}\left(\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\geq N\right)\,.

For some
N
large enough,
Lemma

D.21
yields

(D.5.47)

\prob_{x}\left(\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\geq N\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{B\left(\mathcal{K}_{i},D\right)-\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,.

We now bound
\prob_{x}\left(\sigma_{D_{-\delta_{D}}}<\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\,,\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}<N\right)
for this choice of
N
.
For this, it suffices to note that

\sigma_{D_{-\delta_{D}}}<\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\,,\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}<N
implies that
\dist_{N}\left(x^{\eta},\Gamma_{N}^{\{x\}}\left(\xi-2\varepsilon\right)\right)>{\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2}}
. Then, applying
Corollary

C.2
yields

(D.5.48)

\prob_{x}\left(\sigma_{D_{-\delta_{D}}}<\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\,,\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}<N\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{B\left(\mathcal{K}_{i},D\right)-3\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,,

which concludes the proof.
∎

We can use this lemma to upper-bound the
\mu_{\infty}(D)
.

Lemma D.27
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
,
any measurable set
D
with
\bd D
bounded,
there exists
\eta_{0}>0
such that
for any
0<\eta<\eta_{0}
,

\mu_{\infty}
invariant probability measure for
(x^{\eta}_{n})_{n}
,
for any
i\in I
,

(D.5.49)

\mu_{\infty}(D_{-\delta_{D}})\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\min_{i\in I}\left\{E(\mathcal{K}_{i})+B\left(\mathcal{K}_{i},D\right)\right\}-\min_{i\in I}E(\mathcal{K}_{i})}{\eta}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,,

where

(D.5.50)

D_{-\delta_{D}}\coloneqq\left\{z\in\mathcal{X}:d(z,\mathcal{X}\setminus D)\geq\delta_{D}\right\}\,.

Proof.

Using
\mathcal{V}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{V}_{K}
measurable neighborhoods of
\mathcal{K}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{K}_{K}
small enough given by
Lemma

D.26
, we provide an estimate for the weight of
D
for the unnormalized measure
\widetilde{\mu}
defined in the proof of
Proposition

D.4
, i.e., for

(D.5.51)

\widetilde{\mu}(D_{-\delta_{D}})\coloneqq\int_{\mathcal{V}}\>d\mu_{\mathcal{V}}(x)\ex_{x}\left[\sum_{n=0}^{\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}-1}\one\{x^{\eta}_{n}\in D_{-\delta_{D}}\}\right]\,,

and the result will follow from the estimate on the normalization constant
\widetilde{\mu}(\mathcal{X})
obtained in the proof of
Proposition

D.4
.

Since
\bd D
is bounded,
\bd D_{-\delta_{D}}
is also bounded.
Therefore, by
Lemma

B.2
, there exists
\mathcal{D}
compact set such that, almost surely, if
x^{\eta}_{n-1}\notin D_{-\delta_{D}}
and
x^{\eta}_{n}\in D_{-\delta_{D}}
, then
x^{\eta}_{n}\in\mathcal{D}
.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\mathcal{D}
contains
\cl\mathcal{V}
(choosing
\mathcal{V}
small enough so that it is bounded if necessary).

By
Lemma

D.21
applied with
\mathcal{U}\leftarrow\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{D}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}
, the quantity

(D.5.52)

c\coloneqq\sup\{\ex_{x}[\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}]:x\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}},\,0<\eta\leq\eta_{0}\}

is finite.

Fix
i\in I
and
x\in\mathcal{V}_{i}
. If
\sigma_{D_{-\delta_{D}}}\geq\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}
, then
\sum_{n=0}^{\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}-1}\one\{x^{\eta}_{n}\in D_{-\delta_{D}}\}
would be 0 so, we have that

\displaystyle\ex_{x}\left[\sum_{n=0}^{\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}-1}\one\{x^{\eta}_{n}\in D_{-\delta_{D}}\}\right]

\displaystyle=\ex_{x}\left[\one\{\sigma_{D_{-\delta_{D}}}<\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\}\sum_{n=0}^{\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}-1}\one\{x^{\eta}_{n}\in D_{-\delta_{D}}\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\ex_{x}\left[\one\{\sigma_{D_{-\delta_{D}}}<\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\}\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\right]

(D.5.53)

\displaystyle=\ex_{x}\left[\one\{\sigma_{D_{-\delta_{D}}}<\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\}\left(\sigma_{D_{-\delta_{D}}}+\ex_{x^{\eta}_{\sigma_{D_{-\delta_{D}}}}}\left[\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\right]\right)\right]\,,

where we used the strong Markov property. Bounding
\sigma_{D_{-\delta_{D}}}
by
\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}
on the event
\{\sigma_{D_{-\delta_{D}}}<\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\}
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\ex_{x}\left[\sum_{n=0}^{\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}-1}\one\{x^{\eta}_{n}\in D_{-\delta_{D}}\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\ex_{x}\left[\one\{\sigma_{D_{-\delta_{D}}}<\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\}\left(\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}+\ex_{x^{\eta}_{\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{D_{-\delta_{D}}}}}}}\left[\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\right]\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2c\prob_{x}\left(\sigma_{D_{-\delta_{D}}}<\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\right)

(D.5.54)

\displaystyle\leq 2c\exp\left(-\frac{B\left(\mathcal{K}_{i},D\right)-\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,,

where we used that
x^{\eta}_{\sigma_{D_{-\delta_{D}}}}\in\mathcal{D}
almost surely on the event
\{\sigma_{D_{-\delta_{D}}}<\sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\}
and the definition of
c
for the second inequality and

where we invoked
Lemma

D.26
for the last inequality.

Combining this bound and
Proposition

D.3
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}(D_{-\delta_{D}})

\displaystyle\leq 2c\sum_{i\in I}\mu_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{V}_{i})\exp\left(-\frac{B\left(\mathcal{K}_{i},D\right)-\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)

(D.5.55)

\displaystyle\leq 2c\card I\exp\left(-\frac{\min_{i\in I}\left\{E(\mathcal{K}_{i})+B\left(\mathcal{K}_{i},D\right)\right\}-\min_{i\in I}E(\mathcal{K}_{i})}{\eta}+\frac{2\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,,

which concludes the proof.
∎

D.6.
Convergence and stability

Let us first begin by showing that, for every initial point, the flow of
f
converges to one of the
\mathcal{K}_{i}
.

Lemma D.28
.

For any
x\in\mathcal{X}
, there is
i\in I
such that

(D.6.1)

\lim_{t\to+\infty}d(\Theta_{t}(x),\mathcal{K}_{i})=0.

Proof.

Fix
x\in\mathcal{X}
. For any
t\geq 0
,
\Theta_{t}(x)
belongs to
\{z\in\mathcal{X}:f(z)\leq f(x)\}
, which is compact by coercivity of
f
.
Therefore, the set of accumulation points of
\left(\Theta_{t}(x)\right)_{t\geq 0}
is non-empty, connected and included in
\crit f
. Therefore, since the
\mathcal{K}_{i}
, for
i\in I
, are the connected components of
\crit f
, there is
i\in I
such that the accumulation points of
\left(\Theta_{t}(x)\right)_{t\geq 0}
all belong to
\mathcal{K}_{i}
.

If
d(\Theta_{t}(x),\mathcal{K}_{i})
did not converge to
0
, there would be a subsequence that would converge to some point out of any
\mathcal{K}_{i}
, which would be a contradiction. Therefore,
d(\Theta_{t}(x),\mathcal{K}_{i})
must converge to
0
.
∎

Let us restate the definition of minimizing component that we introduced in the main text.

Definition 9
.

For any
i\in I
, we say that
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is a
minimizing component
if there exists
\mathcal{U}
a neighborhood of
\mathcal{K}_{i}
such that,

(D.6.2)

\argmin_{x\in\mathcal{U}}f(x)=\mathcal{K}_{i}\,.

\mathcal{K}_{i}
is called non-minimizing otherwise.

We now state a standard definition for asymptotic stability.

Definition 10
.

A connected component of the critical points
\mathcal{K}_{i}
, for some
i\in I
, is said to be
asymptotically stable
if there exists
\mathcal{U}
a neighborhood of
\mathcal{K}_{i}
such that, for any
x\in\mathcal{U}
,
\Theta_{t}(x)
converges to
\mathcal{K}_{i}
.

The notions of minimizing component and asymptotic stability are equivalent in our context.

Lemma D.29
.

For any
i\in I
,
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is a minimizing component if and only if it is asymptotically stable.

Proof.

We start with the direct implication.
Assume that
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is a minimizing component. Therefore, there exists
\delta>0
such that

(D.6.3)

\argmin_{x\in\cl\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})}f(x)=\mathcal{K}_{i}\,.

Moreover, assume that
\delta
is small enough so that,
\cl\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})\cap\crit f=\mathcal{K}_{i}
.

Then, for any
x\in\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})\setminus\mathcal{K}_{i}
,
f(x)>f(\mathcal{K}_{i})
.

By continuity of
f
, compactness of
\cl\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})\setminus\mathcal{U}_{\delta/2}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
and definition of minimizing component, we have that

(D.6.4)

\min_{x\in\cl\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})\setminus\mathcal{U}_{\delta/2}(\mathcal{K}_{i})}f(x)>f(\mathcal{K}_{i})\,.

Define
\delta^{\prime}\coloneqq{\frac{1}{2}}\min_{x\in\cl\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})\setminus\mathcal{U}_{\delta/2}(\mathcal{K}_{i})}\left\{f(x)-f(\mathcal{K}_{i})\right\}
and

(D.6.5)

\mathcal{V}\coloneqq\left\{x\in\mathcal{U}_{\delta/2}(\mathcal{K}_{i}):f(x)<f(\mathcal{K}_{i})+\delta^{\prime}\right\}\subset\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})\,,

which is a neighborhood of
\mathcal{K}_{i}
by continuity of
f
.

We now show that trajectories of the flow starting in
\mathcal{V}
converge to
\mathcal{K}_{i}
.
Take
x\in\mathcal{V}
. Since
f(\Theta_{t}(x))
is non-increasing, then
(\Theta_{t}(x))_{t}
remains in
\left\{x\in\mathcal{X}:f(x)<f(\mathcal{K}_{i})+\delta^{\prime}\right\}
.

(\Theta_{t}(x))_{t}
also cannot escape from
\mathcal{U}_{\delta/2}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
: if it were, by continuity of the flow and of the distance to
\mathcal{K}_{i}
, there would be
t>0
such that
d(\Theta_{t}(x),\mathcal{K}_{i})=\delta/2
, which is a contradiction since
f(\Theta_{t}(x))<f(\mathcal{K}_{i})+\delta^{\prime}
. Hence,
(\Theta_{t}(x))_{t}
remains in
\mathcal{U}_{\delta/2}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
and so in
\mathcal{V}
.

By
Lemma

D.28
,
(\Theta_{t}(x))_{t}
converges to some component of the critical points, which must be
\mathcal{K}_{i}
since
\mathcal{V}
is disjoint from the other components.

We now show the converse implication.
Since
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is a connected component of
\crit f
,
f
is constant on
\mathcal{K}_{i}
. Denote by
f_{i}^{\ast}
this value.
Take
\delta>0
small enough such that
\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})\cap\crit f=\mathcal{K}_{i}
and such that, for any
x\in\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
,
(\Theta_{t}(x))_{t}
converges to
\mathcal{K}_{i}
.
Take
x\in\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})\setminus\mathcal{K}_{i}
. We show that
f(x)>f_{i}^{\ast}
.
For any
t>0
,

(D.6.6)

f(\Theta_{t}(x))-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f(x)}=-\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla f(\Theta_{s}(x))\rVert^{2}\>ds\,,

which must (strictly) negative since
x
is not a critical point of
f
. Since
f(\Theta_{t}(x))
is non-increasing in
t
and lower-bounded — because
\inf_{\mathcal{X}}f>-\infty
by coercivity of
f
—, it must converge as
t\to+\infty
and its limit satisfies
\lim_{t\to+\infty}f(\Theta_{t}(x))<f(x)
.
Moreover,
(\Theta_{t}(x))_{t}
converges to
\mathcal{K}_{i}
so that all its accumulation points belong to
\mathcal{K}_{i}
and have the same objective value
f_{i}^{\ast}
. Hence, we have that
\lim_{t\to+\infty}f(\Theta_{t}(x))=f_{i}^{\ast}
and
f_{i}^{\ast}<f(x)
.
∎

In the following, we will thus use the terms minimizing component and asymptotically stable interchangeably.

The next lemma shows that if
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is not asymptotically stable, then it is unstable in the sense of
Freidlin & Wentzell [20, Chap. 6,§4]
.

Lemma D.30
.

If
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is not asymptotically stable, then there exists
j\in I
such that
B_{i,j}=0
.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is not asymptotically stable, then, for every
n\geq 1
, there exists
x_{n}\in\mathcal{U}_{1/n}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
such that
\Theta_{t}(x_{n})
does not converge to
\mathcal{K}_{i}
.
By
Lemma

D.28
, there exists
j_{n}\in I
such that
(\Theta_{t}(x_{n}))_{t}
converges to
\mathcal{K}_{j_{n}}
.
Since
I
is finite, there exists
j\in I
such that
j_{n}=j
for infinitely many
n
.
Replacing
(x_{n})_{n\geq 1}
by a subsequence, we can assume that
(\Theta_{t}(x_{n}))_{t}
converges to
\mathcal{K}_{j}
for all
n\geq 1
.

We now show that
B_{i,j}=0
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
and consider
\mathcal{W}_{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{K}_{i}),\mathcal{W}_{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{K}_{j})
which are neighborhoods of
\mathcal{K}_{i},\mathcal{K}_{j}
by
Lemma

D.8
.
Since
(x_{n})_{n\geq 1}
converges to
\mathcal{K}_{i}
, there exists
n
such that
x_{n}\in\mathcal{W}_{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
.
In turn, since
\Theta_{t}(x_{n})
converges to
\mathcal{K}_{j}
, there exists
T>0
, which we can choose integer, such that,
\Theta_{T}(x_{n})\in\mathcal{W}_{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{K}_{j})
.
Therefore, there exists
x\in\mathcal{K}_{i}
and
z\in\mathcal{K}_{j}
such that
\rho(x,x_{n})<\varepsilon
and
\rho(\Theta_{T}(x_{n}),z)<\varepsilon
.
Consider the discrete path
\xi\in\mathcal{X}^{T+3}
defined by

\xi_{0}=x
,

\xi_{n}=\Theta_{n-1}(x)
for all
1\leq n\leq T+1
and
\xi_{T+2}=z
.
By
Lemma

D.2
, we have that

(D.6.7)

\mathcal{A}_{T+3}(\xi)=\rho(x,x_{n})+\rho(\Theta_{T}(x),z)<2\varepsilon\,,

and therefore, since
\xi_{0}\in\mathcal{K}_{i}
and
\xi_{T+2}\in\mathcal{K}_{j}
,
B_{i,j}<2\varepsilon
.
∎

The following lemma is now a straightforward adaptation of
Freidlin & Wentzell [20, Chap. 6, Lemma 4.2]
.

Lemma D.31
.

If
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is not asymptotically stable, then there exists
j\in I
such that
B_{i,j}=0
and such that, for any
l\in I
,
B_{j,l}>0
.

Proof.

For the sake of contradiction, assume that such a
j
does not exist.
Then we build an infinite sequence
j_{0}=i,j_{1},\dots
such that
B_{j_{n},j_{n+1}}=0
for all
n\geq 0
.
By definition of equivalence classes, any
j
cannot appear twice in this sequence. But since
I
is finite, this is a contradiction.
∎

We now reach our final result on unstable equivalence classes: they have negligible weight in the invariant measure.

Lemma D.32
.

If
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is not asymptotically stable, or, equivalently, non-minimizing, then there exists
j\in I
such that
\mathcal{K}_{j}
is asymptotically stable,
B_{i,j}=0
and,

(D.6.8)

E_{j}<E_{i}

Proof.

By
Lemma

D.31
, there exists
j\in I
such that
B_{i,j}=0
and such that, for any
l\in I
,
B_{j,l}>0
.

Lemma

D.30
implies in particular that
\mathcal{K}_{j}
must be asymptotically stable

It remains to show that
E_{j}<E_{i}
.
Take
T\in\mathcal{T}_{i}
such that

(D.6.9)

E_{i}=\sum_{(l\to k)\in T}B_{l,k}\,.

j
has an outgoing edge in
T
and denote by
j^{\prime}
the other end of that edge.
Now, consider the tree
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{j}
obtained from
T
by removing the outgoing edge from
j
to
i^{\prime}
and adding an edge from
i
to
j
.
Then, by definition of
E_{j}
,

(D.6.10)

\displaystyle E_{j}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{(l\to k)\in T^{\prime}}B_{l,k}=\sum_{(l\to k)\in T}B_{l,k}-B_{j,j^{\prime}}+B_{i,j}\,.

But, by definition of
j
,
B_{i,j}=0
and
B_{j,j^{\prime}}>0
. Therefore,
E_{j}<E_{i}
.
∎

We now show the second part of our main result: the invariant measure concentrates on the ground states, which are asymptotically stable by
Lemma

D.32
.

For this, we need the following lemma.

Lemma D.33
.

For any
i\in I
such that
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is minimizing, there exists
\delta>0
such that, for any
0<\delta^{\prime}\leq\delta
,

(D.6.11)

B(\mathcal{K}_{i},\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathcal{K}_{i}))>0\,.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is minimizing, there exists
\delta>0
such that, for any
x\in\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
,
f(x)>f_{i}
where
f_{i}
is the value of
f
on
\mathcal{K}_{i}
.
Take
\delta^{\prime}\leq\delta
,
\mathcal{U}\coloneqq\mathcal{U}_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
and

(D.6.12)

\Delta\coloneqq\min\left\{U_{\infty}(x)-\alpha_{\infty}(f_{i}):x\in\mathcal{X},\,d(x,\mathcal{K}_{i})=\delta^{\prime}/2\right\}\,.

Then, by the continuity of
U_{\infty}
and the fact that
\alpha_{\infty}
is (strictly) increasing, we have that
\Delta>0
.
To conclude the proof of this lemma, we now show that
B(\mathcal{K}_{i},\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{U})\geq{\frac{\Delta}{2}}
.
Consider some
T>0
and
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}([0,T],\mathcal{X})
such that
\gamma_{0}\in\mathcal{K}_{i}
and
\gamma_{T}\in\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{W}
.
By continuity of
\gamma
and
d(\cdot,\mathcal{K}_{i})
, there exists
t\in[0,T]
such that
d(\gamma_{t},\mathcal{K}_{i})=\delta^{\prime}/2
.
By the same computation as in
Lemma

D.6
, we have that

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle\leq U_{\infty}(\gamma_{t})-U_{\infty}(\gamma_{0})

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\langle\dot{\gamma}_{s},\nabla f(\gamma_{s})\rangle}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(\gamma_{s}))}

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathcal{S}_{[0,t]}(\gamma)

(D.6.13)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathcal{S}_{[0,T]}(\gamma)\,.

Since this is valid for any
\gamma
, we obtain that
B(\mathcal{K}_{i},\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{U})\geq{\frac{\Delta}{2}}
.
∎

The next proposition shows that the invariant measure concentrates exponentially on states that are asymptotically stable (and contain the ground states).

Proposition D.5
.

Consider
J\subset I
such that, for all
i\in J
,
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is minimizing and, such that,
J
contains
\argmin_{i\in I}E_{i}
.
Consider
\mathcal{V}_{i}
small enough neighborhoods of
\mathcal{K}_{i}
for
i\in J
.
Then, there exists
c>0
,
\eta_{0}>0
such that, for any
\eta\leq\eta_{0}
, for any
\mu_{\infty}
invariant measure of
(x^{\eta}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
,

(D.6.14)

\mu_{\infty}\left(\mathcal{X}\setminus\bigcup_{i\in J}\mathcal{V}_{i}\right)\leq e^{-\frac{c}{\eta}}\,.

Proof.

Take
\delta>0
small enough so that, for any
i\in J
,

\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})\subset\mathcal{V}_{i}
and

(D.6.15)

d(\mathcal{K}_{i},\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i}))>0\,.

This is possible by
Lemma

D.33
.

Define

(D.6.16)

D\coloneqq\mathcal{X}\setminus\bigcup_{i\in J}\mathcal{U}_{\delta/2}(\mathcal{K}_{i})\,.

With the notations of
Lemma

D.27
, we show that

(D.6.17)

\mathcal{X}\setminus\bigcup_{i\in J}\mathcal{V}_{i}\subset D_{-\delta/2}\,.

Indeed, take
x\in\mathcal{X}\setminus\bigcup_{i\in J}\mathcal{V}_{i}
.
Then, for any
i\in J
, it holds that
d(x,\mathcal{K}_{i})\geq\delta
and so, we have

(D.6.18)

d(x,\mathcal{X}\setminus D)=d\left(x,\mathcal{X}\setminus\bigcup_{i\in J}\mathcal{U}_{\delta/2}(\mathcal{K}_{i})\right)\geq\frac{\delta}{2}\,.

Hence
x\in D_{-\delta/2}
and, it suffices to bound
\mu_{\infty}(D_{-\delta/2})
to show the result.

Moreover, for any
i\in J
,

(D.6.19)

B(\mathcal{K}_{i},D)\geq B(\mathcal{K}_{i},\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{\delta/2}(\mathcal{K}_{i}))>0\,,

so that the quantity

(D.6.20)

c\coloneqq\min\left(\min_{i\notin J}E_{i}-\min_{i\in I}E_{i},\min_{i\in J}B(\mathcal{K}_{i},D)\right)

is positive.

Apply
Lemma

D.27
with
\delta_{D}\leftarrow\delta/2
,
\varepsilon\leftarrow c/2
to get that, for any
\eta\leq\eta_{0}
,

(D.6.21)

\mu_{\infty}(D_{-\delta/2})\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\min_{i\in I}\left\{E(\mathcal{K}_{i})+B\left(\mathcal{K}_{i},D\right)\right\}-\min_{j\in I}E(\mathcal{K}_{j})}{\eta}+\frac{c}{2\eta}\right)\,.

But, for any
i\in I
, the exponent can be estimated as

(D.6.22)

\left\{E(\mathcal{K}_{i})+B\left(\mathcal{K}_{i},D\right)\right\}-\min_{j\in I}E(\mathcal{K}_{i})\geq\begin{cases}E(\mathcal{K}_{i})-\min_{j\in I}E(\mathcal{K}_{j})&\text{if }i\notin J\\
B(\mathcal{K}_{i},D)&\text{if }i\in J\,,\end{cases}

which is always positive, even in the first case, since
\argmin_{j\in I}E(\mathcal{K}_{j})\subset J
.
Therefore, it holds that

(D.6.23)

\min_{i\in I}\left\{E(\mathcal{K}_{i})+B\left(\mathcal{K}_{i},D\right)\right\}-\min_{j\in I}E(\mathcal{K}_{j})-\varepsilon\geq c-\varepsilon=\frac{c}{2}\,,

which concludes the proof.
∎

D.7.
Main results

In this section, we restate the main results
Theorems

1
,
2
,
3
and
4

and provide their proofs. They are now mostly corollaries of the results of
Sections

D.5
and
D.6
.

Theorem D.1
.

Suppose that
\mu_{\infty}
is invariant under (
SGD
),
fix a tolerance level
\varepsilon>0
,
and
let
\mathcal{U}_{i}\equiv\mathcal{U}_{i}(\delta)
,
i=1,\dotsc,K
, be
\delta
-neighborhoods of the components of
\crit f
.
Then, for all sufficiently small
\delta,\eta>0
, we have

(D.7.1)

\left\lvert\eta\log\mu_{\infty}(\mathcal{U}_{i})+E_{i}-\min\nolimits_{j}E_{j}\right\rvert\leq\varepsilon

and

(D.7.2)

\left\lvert\eta\log\frac{\mu_{\infty}(\mathcal{U}_{i})}{\mu_{\infty}(\mathcal{U}_{j})}+E_{i}-E_{j}\right\rvert\leq\varepsilon.

More compactly, with notation as above, we have:

(D.7.3)

\mu_{\infty}(\mathcal{U}_{i})\propto\exp\left(-\frac{E_{i}+\bigoh(\varepsilon)}{\eta}\right).

Proof.

Note that if
\mu_{\infty}
is invariant for (
SGD
), it is
a fortiori
invariant for the accelerated process
(x^{\eta}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
.
This result is then a direct consequence of
Proposition

D.4
(
Section

D.5
).
∎

Theorem D.2
.

Suppose that
\mu_{\infty}
is invariant under (
SGD
),
and let
\mathcal{K}
be a non-minimizing component of
f
.
Then, with notation as in
Theorem

1
, there exists a minimizing component
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
of
f
and a positive constant
c\equiv c(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\prime})>0
such that

(D.7.4)

\frac{\mu_{\infty}(\mathcal{U})}{\mu_{\infty}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})}\leq\exp\left(-\frac{c(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\prime})+\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)

for all all sufficiently small
\eta>0
and all sufficiently small neighborhoods
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
respectively.
In particular, in the limit
\eta\to 0
, we have
\mu_{\infty}(\mathcal{U})\to 0
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{i}
be a non-minimizing component. By
Lemma

D.32
, there exists
j\in I
such that
E_{j}<E_{i}
. The statement then follows from
Theorem

1
.
∎

Theorem D.3
.

Suppose that
\mu_{\infty}
is invariant under (
SGD
),
fix a tolerance level
\delta>0
,
and
let
\mathcal{U}\equiv\mathcal{U}(\delta)
be a
\delta
-neighborhood of
\crit f
.
Then there exists a constant
c\equiv c_{\delta}>0
such that, for all sufficiently small
\eta>0
, we have:

(D.7.5)

\mu_{\infty}(\mathcal{U})\geq 1-e^{-c/\eta}.

Proof.

We actually show a slighly stronger result. Define
J\coloneqq\left\{i\in I:\mathcal{K}_{i}\text{ is minimizing}\right\}
.
We prove that there exists
\mathcal{U}
neighborhood of
\bigcup_{i\in J}\mathcal{K}_{i}
, a constant
c>0
, such that, for all
\eta
small enough,

(D.7.6)

\mu_{\infty}\left(\mathcal{X}\setminus\bigcup_{i\in J}\mathcal{V}_{i}\right)\leq e^{-\frac{c}{\eta}}\,.

This is then a consequence of
Proposition

D.5
(
Section

D.6
) with
J\leftarrow J
. Note
J
contain the ground states since they are minimizing by
Lemma

D.32
.
∎

Theorem D.4
.

Suppose that
\mu_{\infty}
is invariant under (
SGD
),
fix a tolerance level
\delta>0
,
and
let
\mathcal{U}_{0}\equiv\mathcal{U}_{0}(\delta)
be a
\delta
-neighborhood of the system’s ground state
\mathcal{K}_{0}
.
Then there exists a constant
c\equiv c_{\delta}>0
such that, for all sufficiently small
\eta>0
, we have:

(D.7.7)

\mu_{\infty}(\mathcal{U}_{0})\geq 1-e^{-c/\eta}.

Proof.

It suffices to apply
Proposition

D.5
(
Section

D.6
) with
J
as the set of ground states
\argmin_{i\in I}E_{i}
.
They are necessarily minimizing by
Lemma

D.32
.
∎

D.8.
Extension: the mean occupation measures

We now state and prove analogue of
Theorems

1
,
2
,
3
and
4
for the mean occupation measure
\mu_{n}
.

For this we will need a strengthened version of
Assumption

∗
, viz.

Assumption
3
∗∗

(Variant of
Assumption

∗
)
.

The
SNR
of
\orcl
satisfies

(
4
∗∗
)

\frac{\lVert\nabla f(x)\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x))}\to\infty\quad\text{as}\quad\lVert x\rVert\to\infty\,.

In this section, we will posit that
Assumption

∗∗
holds in addition to

Assumptions

∗
,
∗
‣
B.2
and
4
.
We then have the following series of results for the occupation measure
\mu_{n}
of (
SGD
).

Theorem
1
∗

(Occupation variant of
Theorem

1
)
.

Fix a tolerance level
\varepsilon>0
,
and
let
\mathcal{U}_{i}\equiv\mathcal{U}_{i}(\delta)
,
i=1,\dotsc,K
, be
\delta
-neighborhoods of the components of
\crit f
.
Then, for all sufficiently small
\delta,\eta>0
and large enough
n
, we have

(D.8.1)

\left\lvert\eta\log\mu_{n}(\mathcal{U}_{i})+E_{i}-\min\nolimits_{j}E_{j}\right\rvert\leq\varepsilon

and

(D.8.2)

\left\lvert\eta\log\frac{\mu_{n}(\mathcal{U}_{i})}{\mu_{n}(\mathcal{U}_{j})}+E_{i}-E_{j}\right\rvert\leq\varepsilon.

More compactly, with notation as above, we have:

(D.8.3)

\mu_{n}(\mathcal{U}_{i})\propto\exp\left(-\frac{E_{i}+\bigoh(\varepsilon)}{\eta}\right).

Theorem
2
∗

(Occupation variant of
Theorem

2
)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a non-minimizing component of
f
.
Then, with notation as in
Theorem

1
, there exists a minimizing component
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
of
f
and a positive constant
c\equiv c(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\prime})>0
such that

(D.8.4)

\frac{\mu_{n}(\mathcal{U})}{\mu_{n}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})}\leq\exp\left(-\frac{c(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\prime})+\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)

for all all sufficiently small
\eta>0
,
n
large enough and all sufficiently small neighborhoods
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
respectively.

Theorem
3
∗

(Occupation variant of
Theorem

3
)
.

Fix a tolerance level
\delta>0
,
and
let
\mathcal{U}\equiv\mathcal{U}(\delta)
be a
\delta
-neighborhood of
\crit f
.
Then there exists a constant
c\equiv c_{\delta}>0
such that, for all sufficiently small
\eta>0
and large enough
n
, we have:

(D.8.5)

\mu_{n}(\mathcal{U})\geq 1-e^{-c/\eta}.

Theorem
4
∗

(Occupation variant of
Theorem

4
)
.

Fix a tolerance level
\delta>0
,
and
let
\mathcal{U}_{0}\equiv\mathcal{U}_{0}(\delta)
be a
\delta
-neighborhood of the system’s ground state
\mathcal{K}_{0}
.
Then there exists a constant
c\equiv c_{\delta}>0
such that, for all sufficiently small
\eta>0
and large enough
n
, we have:

(D.8.6)

\mu_{n}(\mathcal{U}_{0})\geq 1-e^{-c/\eta}.

We begin with a preliminary lemma which shows that, under
Assumption

∗∗
, the sequence of mean occupation measure
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is tight (see e.g.,
Kallenberg [32, Chap. 23]
).

Lemma D.34
.

The sequence of mean occupation measures
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is tight.

The proof of this lemma first follows the proof of
Lemma

D.16
and then relies on the same reasoning as the proof of
Douc et al. [14, Thm. 12.3.3]
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

D.14
, there exists
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{X}
compact,
\eta_{0}>0
,
c>0
such that, for any
\eta\leq\eta_{0}
,
x_{0}=x\notin\mathcal{K}
,

(D.8.7)

U_{\infty}(x_{1})-U_{\infty}(x)\leq\eta\left(\frac{\lVert\mathsf{U}(x,\omega_{0})\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x))}-\frac{\lVert\nabla f(x)\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x))}\right)\,.

Passing to the expectation yields that, for any
x\notin\mathcal{K}
,

(D.8.8)

\ex_{x}\left[U_{\infty}(x_{1})\right]-{U_{\infty}(x)}\leq\eta\left(\frac{\ex_{x}\left[\lVert\mathsf{U}(x,\omega_{0})\rVert^{2}\right]}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x))}-\frac{\lVert\nabla f(x)\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x))}\right)\,.

Appplying
Corollary

D.2
with
X\leftarrow\frac{\mathsf{U}(x,\omega_{0})}{\sqrt{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x))}}
(the conditions of application are verified from
Assumption

∗
Item

(c.5pt)
) yields that

(D.8.9)

\ex_{x}\left[U_{\infty}(x_{1})\right]-{U_{\infty}(x)}\leq-\eta\frac{\lVert\nabla f(x)\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x))}+\eta\times 16d\log 6\,.

Hence, for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
, we have

\displaystyle\ex_{x}\left[U_{\infty}(x_{1})\right]-U_{\infty}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\one\{x\in\mathcal{K}\}\left(\sup_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}}\ex_{x^{\prime}}[U_{\infty}(x^{\prime})]-\inf_{\mathcal{X}}U_{\infty}\right)

(D.8.10)

\displaystyle-\one\{x\notin\mathcal{K}\}\left(\eta\frac{\lVert\nabla f(x)\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x))}-\eta\times 16d\log 6\right)\,,

or, after rearranging,

\displaystyle\ex_{x}\left[U_{\infty}(x_{1})\right]

\displaystyle-\one\{x\in\mathcal{K}\}\left(\sup_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}}\ex_{x^{\prime}}[U_{\infty}(x^{\prime})]-\inf_{\mathcal{X}}U_{\infty}\right)

(D.8.11)

\displaystyle+\one\{x\notin\mathcal{K}\}\left(\eta\frac{\lVert\nabla f(x)\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x))}-\eta\times 16d\log 6\right)\leq U_{\infty}(x)\,.

Since the function

\displaystyle x\mapsto

\displaystyle-\one\{x\in\mathcal{K}\}\left(\sup_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}}\ex_{x^{\prime}}[U_{\infty}(x^{\prime})]-\inf_{\mathcal{X}}U_{\infty}\right)

(D.8.12)

\displaystyle+\one\{x\notin\mathcal{K}\}\left(\eta\frac{\lVert\nabla f(x)\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\infty}^{2}(f(x))}-\eta\times 16d\log 6\right)

is measurable, lower-bounded and goes to infinity as
\lVert x\rVert\to\infty
by
Assumption

∗∗
, one can then apply the same computations as in the proof of
Douc et al. [14, Thm. 12.3.3]
to obtain that the sequence of occupation measures
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is tight.
∎

We now prove
Theorem

∗
by adapting the proof of
Theorem

1
.
Since the process is exactly the same for
Theorems

∗
,
∗
‣
D.8
and
∗
‣
D.8
, we omit their proofs.

Proof of
Theorem

∗
.

We show that, for sufficiently small
\delta,\eta>0
, for any
i\in I
, we have that

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{E_{i}-\min\nolimits_{j}E_{j}+\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}(\mathcal{U}_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}(\mathcal{U}_{i})

(D.8.13)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{E_{i}-\min\nolimits_{j}E_{j}-\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)

and the results in the statement will follow with
2\varepsilon
in place of
\varepsilon
.

We apply
Proposition

D.4
(
Section

D.5
): take
\delta
small enough so

Proposition

D.4
can be applied with both the neighborhoods
\mathcal{U}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{U}_{K}
and
\cl\mathcal{U}_{1},\dots,\cl\mathcal{U}_{K}
.
One then obtain
\eta_{0}>0
such that, for all
0<\eta<\eta_{0}
and any
\mu_{\infty}
invariant probability measure for
(x^{\eta}_{n})_{n}
,
for any
i\in I
,

(D.8.14)

\displaystyle\mu_{\infty}(\mathcal{U}_{i})

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(-\frac{E(\mathcal{K}_{i})-\min_{j\in I}E(\mathcal{K}_{j})}{\eta}-\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)

\displaystyle\mu_{\infty}(\cl\mathcal{U}_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{E(\mathcal{K}_{i})-\min_{j\in I}E(\mathcal{K}_{j})}{\eta}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)\,.

We now prove that
Eq.

D.8.13
holds.
Fix
i\in I
. By
Lemma

D.34
, the sequence of mean occupation measures
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is tight, so that, by Prohorov theorem
[
32
, Thm. 23.2]
, it is sequentially compact for the weak topology, or, in other terms, for the convergence in distribution.
Therefore,
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 0}
admits a weak accumulation point which is a probability distribution and that we denote by
\nu
.
Applying Portmanteau theorem
[
32
, Thm. 5.25]
to the open set
\mathcal{U}_{i}
and the closed set
\cl\mathcal{U}_{i}
yields that

(D.8.15)

\nu(\mathcal{U}_{i})\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}(\mathcal{U}_{i})\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}(\cl\mathcal{U}_{i})\leq\nu(\cl\mathcal{U}_{i})\,.

Since
(x_{n})_{n\geq 0}
, the sequence of iterates of
SGD
, is (weak) Feller by
Lemma

D.15
,
\nu
is actually invariant for
(x_{n})_{n\geq 0}
, by, e.g.,
Douc et al. [14, Prop. 12.3.1]
, and

a fortiori
invariant for the accelerated process
(x^{\eta}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
.
Combining
Eq.

D.8.14
with
Eq.

D.8.15
gives the result
Eq.

D.8.13
.
∎

Appendix E
Potential for the invariant measure

E.1.
Gaussian noise

Though it does not formally fit into our setting, let us first begin with the case where the noise is Gaussian. Since it is unbounded, our assumptions are not satisfied and our theorems describing the invariant measure do not apply. However, all the objects we consider are still well-defined, and, in that case, it is possible to compute the
E_{i}
explicitly. Moreover, this section serves as a blueprint for the truncated Gaussian case of the next section.

Assume that, for every
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
\mathsf{U}(x,\omega)
follows a centered Gaussian distribution with covariance
\sigma^{2}(f(x))I
for some continuous function
\sigma^{2}:\mathbb{R}\to(0,+\infty)
.

Akin to
U_{\infty}
in
Appendix

D
, a key role is played by the function
U:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

(E.1.1)

U(x)\coloneqq 2\alpha(f(x))\quad\text{ with }\quad\alpha\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\quad\text{ a primitive of }\quad 1/{\sigma^{2}}\,.

Since the noise is Gaussian, the Lagrangian and Hamiltonian have explicit expressions: for every
x,p,v\in\mathcal{X}
,

(E.1.2a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(x,p)

\displaystyle=-\langle\nabla f(x),p\rangle+{\frac{1}{2}}\sigma^{2}(f(x))\lVert p\rVert^{2}

(E.1.2b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(x,v)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lVert v+\nabla f(x)\rVert}{2\sigma^{2}(f(x))}\,.

This expression of
\mathcal{L}
make it clear that the action function penalizes the deviation of a path from the flow: for a path
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}([0,T])
for some
T>0
,

(E.1.3)

\mathcal{S}_{T}(\gamma)=\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\lVert\dot{\gamma}_{t}+\nabla f(\gamma_{t})\rVert^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}(f(\gamma_{t}))}\>dt\,.

The computation of the
E_{i}
relies on the following observation.
Take a path
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}([0,T])
for some
T>0
and consider
\varphi
defined by
\varphi_{t}=\gamma_{T-t}
for
t\in[0,T]
. Then, the action cost of
\varphi
is given by,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{T}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\lVert-\dot{\gamma}_{t}+\nabla f(\gamma_{t})\rVert^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}(f(\gamma_{t}))}\>dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\lVert\dot{\gamma}_{t}+\nabla f(\gamma_{t})\rVert^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}(f(\gamma_{t}))}\>dt-\int_{0}^{T}\frac{2\langle\dot{\gamma}_{t},\nabla f(\gamma_{t})\rangle}{\sigma^{2}(f(\gamma_{t}))}\>dt

(E.1.4)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}_{T}(\gamma)-\int_{0}^{T}\langle\dot{\gamma}_{t},\nabla U(\gamma_{t})\rangle\>dt\,,

since
\nabla U(x)=2\alpha^{\prime}(f(x))\nabla f(x)
.
Therefore, we get that

(E.1.5)

\mathcal{S}_{T}(\varphi)=\mathcal{S}_{T}(\gamma)-(U(\gamma_{T})-U(\gamma_{0}))\,.

Take
i,j\in I
. This equality then translates to a relation between
B_{i,j}
and
B_{j,i}
: considering
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}([0,T])
such that
\gamma_{0}\in\mathcal{K}_{i}
,
\gamma_{T}\in\mathcal{K}_{j}
and taking the infimum over all such paths, we get that

(E.1.6)

B_{j,i}\leq B_{i,j}+(U_{j}-U_{i})\,.

where, since
f
is constant on
\mathcal{K}_{i}
and
\mathcal{K}_{j}
, we denote by
U_{i}
and
U_{j}
the values of
U
on
\mathcal{K}_{i}
and
\mathcal{K}_{j}
respectively.

Reversing the roles of
i
and
j
and applying the same argument shows that this inequality is an equality:

(E.1.7)

B_{j,i}+U_{j}=B_{i,j}+U_{i}\,.

Denote by
C_{i,j}
this common value. Crucially,
C_{i,j}
is symmetric in
i
and
j
.

Consider now
T^{\ast}
a minimum weight spanning tree on the complete but now undirected graph on
I
with weights
(C_{i,j})_{i,j}
. We show that the minima in the
E_{i}
are attained
T^{\ast}
, or more precisely, a directed version of it.

Fix
i
and consider
T\in\mathcal{T}_{i}
a spanning tree rooted at
i
.
We have that, since any node
j
is the origin of exactly one edge in
T
,

\displaystyle\sum_{(j\to l)\in T}B_{j,l}+\sum_{j\in I}U_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(j\to l)\in T}\left(B_{j,l}+U_{j}\right)+U_{i}

(E.1.8)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(j\to l)\in T}C_{j,l}+U_{i}\,.

But by definition of
T^{\ast}
, this sum is at least greater than
\sum_{(j\leftrightarrow l)\in T^{\ast}}C_{j,l}
so that we have

(E.1.9)

\sum_{(j\to l)\in T}B_{j,l}+\sum_{j\in I}U_{j}\geq\sum_{(j\leftrightarrow l)\in T^{\ast}}C_{j,l}+U_{i}\,,

and taking
T_{i}
an oriented version of
T^{\ast}
rooted at
i
, the equality is attained.
Therefore, we have that

(E.1.10)

E_{i}=\sum_{(j\to l)\in T_{i}}B_{j,l}=\sum_{(j\leftrightarrow l)\in T^{\ast}}C_{j,l}-\sum_{j\in I}U_{j}+U_{i}\,,

or, in short,
E_{i}=U_{i}+c
where
c>0
is independent of
i
.

Therefore, the mass distribution over critical points is governed by a Gibbs measure with potential
U
.

Let us now mention two particular cases.

•

If
\sigma^{2}
is constant, then
U=\frac{2f}{\sigma^{2}}
.

•

It
\sigma^{2}
is linear, i.e., of the form
\sigma^{2}(f(x))=\sigma^{2}_{1}(f(x)+\sigma^{2}_{0})
, then
U=\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}_{1}}\log(f+\sigma^{2}_{0})
.

E.2.
Truncated Gaussian noise

To fit into our theoretical framework, we consider truncated Gaussian noise instead. The general outline of the proof but with added steps to handle the error due to the truncation. In particular, one must show that, without loss of generality, we can only conider paths whose derivative has the same norm as the gradient of
f
. This is done with
Freidlin & Wentzell [20, Chap. 4, Lem. 3.1]
that we adapt to our setting.

Assume that
\mathsf{U}(x,\omega)
follows a centered Gaussian distribution with covariance
\sigma^{2}(f(x))I
conditioned on being in
\mathbb{B}(0,R(x))
for some
R(x)>0
.

As in
Definition

4
, we define
U(x)=2\alpha(f(x))
with
\alpha^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}
and denote by
U_{i}
the value taken by
U
on
\mathcal{K}_{i}
.

Consider some
0<{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\leq{\frac{1}{2}}
and assume that

(E.2.1)

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}2^{d+4}(d+1)e^{-\tfrac{R^{2}}{16\sigma^{2}}}\leq{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\,,

so that the error term
2E(\sigma^{2}(f(x)),R(x))
in
Lemma

F.2
is bounded by
{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
.

Moreover, assume that, for any
x\in\mathcal{X}

(E.2.2)

\lVert\nabla f(x)\rVert\leq\frac{R(x)}{8}

Lemma E.1
.

Consider
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}([0,T])
.
Then, there exists
\widetilde{\gamma}\in\mathcal{C}([0,S])
a reparametrization of
\gamma
such that, for any
t\in[0,S]
,

(E.2.3)

\lVert\dot{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{s}\rVert=\lVert\nabla f(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s})\rVert\,.

and

(E.2.4)

\mathcal{S}_{T}(\gamma)\geq\int_{0}^{S}\frac{\lVert\dot{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{s}+\nabla f(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s})\rVert^{2}}{2(1+{\varepsilon^{\prime}})\sigma^{2}(f(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s}))}\>ds\,.

Proof.

By the proof
Freidlin & Wentzell [20, Chap. 4, Lem. 3.1]
, there exists
t(s)
change of time such that, with
\widetilde{\gamma}_{s}=\gamma_{t(s)}
,
\lVert\dot{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{s}\rVert=\lVert\nabla f(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s})\rVert
.

We have that

(E.2.5)

\mathcal{S}_{T}(\gamma)=\int_{0}^{t^{-1}(T)}\dot{t}(s)\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s},(\dot{t}(s))^{-1}\dot{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{s})\>ds\,,

so it suffices to bound
\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s},\dot{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{s})
from below: by definition, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s},(\dot{t}(s))^{-1}\dot{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{s})

\displaystyle\geq\sup\left\{\langle p,(\dot{t}(s))^{-1}\dot{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}+\nabla f(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s})\rangle-\bar{\mathcal{H}}(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s},p):\lVert p\rVert\leq\frac{R(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s})}{2\sigma^{2}(f(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s}))}\right\}

(E.2.6)

\displaystyle\geq\sup\left\{\langle p,(\dot{t}(s))^{-1}\dot{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}+\nabla f(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s})\rangle-(1+{\varepsilon^{\prime}}){\frac{\sigma^{2}(f(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s}))}{2}}\lVert p\rVert^{2}:\lVert p\rVert\leq\frac{R(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s})}{2\sigma^{2}(f(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s}))}\right\}\,,

by
Lemma

F.2
.
Applying
Lemma

F.3
with
v\leftarrow(\dot{t}(s))^{-1}\dot{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{t}
,
w\leftarrow\nabla f(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s})
and
\lambda\leftarrow(\dot{t}(s))^{-1}
,
now exactly yields, for almost all
s
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s},(\dot{t}(s))^{-1}\dot{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{s})

\displaystyle\geq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\dot{t}(s))^{-1}}\sup\left\{\langle p,\dot{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}}+\nabla f(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s})\rangle-(1+{\varepsilon^{\prime}}){\frac{\sigma^{2}(f(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s}))}{2}}\lVert p\rVert^{2}:\lVert p\rVert\leq\frac{R(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s})}{2\sigma^{2}(f(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s}))}\right\}

(E.2.7)

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\dot{t}(s))^{-1}}\frac{\lVert\dot{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{s}+\nabla f(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s})\rVert^{2}}{2(1+{\varepsilon^{\prime}})\sigma^{2}(f(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s}))}\,,

since
\lVert\dot{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{s}+\nabla f(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s})\rVert\leq\frac{R(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s})}{2}
.
∎

Lemma E.2
.

With this setting, for any
i\in I

(E.2.8)

E_{i}=\sum_{(j\leftrightarrow l)\in T^{\ast}}C_{j,l}-\sum_{j\in I}U_{j}+U_{i}\,,

Proof.

Consider
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}([0,T])
such that
\gamma_{0}\in\mathcal{K}_{i}
,
\gamma_{T}\in\mathcal{K}_{j}
and
\mathcal{S}_{T}(\gamma)<+\infty
.
Then, by the previous lemma
Lemma

E.1
, there exists
\widetilde{\gamma}\in\mathcal{C}([0,S])
a reparametrization of
\gamma
such that, for any
t\in[0,S]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{T}(\gamma)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{S}\frac{\lVert\dot{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{s}+\nabla f(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s})\rVert^{2}}{2(1+{\varepsilon^{\prime}})\sigma^{2}(f(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s}))}\>ds

(E.2.9)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{S}\frac{\lVert-\dot{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{s}+\nabla f(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s})\rVert^{2}}{2(1+{\varepsilon^{\prime}})\sigma^{2}(f(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s}))}\>ds+\frac{U(\gamma_{T})-U(\gamma_{0})}{1+{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}\,,

where we performed the same computations as above in
Section

E.1
.
Considering the path
(\widetilde{\gamma}_{S-s})_{s\in[0,S]}
and invoking the upper-bound on the Lagrangian from
Lemma

F.2
with
-\dot{\widetilde{\gamma}}_{s}+\nabla f(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s})
which still has norm less than
R(\widetilde{\gamma}_{s})/4
, we get that

(E.2.10)

\mathcal{S}_{T}(\gamma)\geq\frac{1-{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}{1+{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}B_{j,i}+\frac{U(\gamma_{T})-U(\gamma_{0})}{1+{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}\,.

The result now follows from the same computations as in
Section

E.1
.
∎

E.3.
Local dependencies

Under local assumptions similar to
Mori et al. [57]
, we demonstrate how the modelling of the noise influences the invariant measure.

Lemma E.3
.

Consider
\mathcal{K}_{i}
minimizing,

\sigma^{2}\colon\mathbb{R}\to(0,+\infty)
continuous, and take
\alpha\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\alpha^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}
.
Assume that
H^{\ast}
is a positive definite matrix such that, locally near
\mathcal{K}_{i}
, it holds that:

(E.3.1)

\alpha(f(x))=\sum_{\lambda\in\eig H^{\ast}}g^{\lambda}(x_{\lambda})\,,

where
x_{\lambda}
denotes the orthogonal projection of
x
on the eigenspace of the eigenvalue
\lambda
and where
g^{\lambda}\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
is continuously differentiable.
Define the potential
U\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
by

(E.3.2)

U(x)=\sum_{\lambda\in\eig H^{\ast}}\frac{2g^{\lambda}(x_{\lambda})}{\lambda}\,.

If we have the anistropic subGaussian bound: for
x
in a neighborhood of
\mathcal{K}_{i}
,
p\in\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,\lVert U(x)\rVert)
,

(E.3.3)

\bar{\mathcal{H}}(x,p)\leq\frac{\sigma^{2}(f(x))}{2}\langle p,H^{\ast}p\rangle\,,

then there is
\delta>0
such that, for all
j\neq i
, any
0<\delta^{\prime}\leq\delta
,

(E.3.4)

E_{j}\geq\min\left\{U(x)-U_{i}:x,d(x,\mathcal{K}_{i})=\delta^{\prime}\right\}>0\,,

where
U_{i}
is the value of
U
on
\mathcal{K}_{i}
.

Moreover, there exists
R>\delta
such that, if there exists
c>0
,
s^{2}>0
, such that, for all
x\in\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,R)\setminus\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
,
v\in\mathbb{B}(\nabla f(x),c)
,

(E.3.5)

\bar{\mathcal{L}}(x,v)\leq\frac{\lVert v\rVert}{2s^{2}}\,,

then there exists
C>0
that depends only on
r,R,c
,
f
restricted to
\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{r}(\mathcal{K}_{i})

such that

(E.3.6)

E_{i}\leq\frac{C}{s^{2}}\,.

The assumptions on the Hamiltonian and the Lagrangian roughly say that the noise share some similarities with Gaussian distributions with the prescribed variances. In particular, they are satisfied in the (truncated) Gaussian case, as shown in
Lemma

F.2
.

Moreover, a takeaway of this lemma is that, if
\sigma^{2}
or the eigenvalues of
H^{\ast}
are small enough, then
\mathcal{K}_{i}
must be the ground state even if it may not be the global minimum of
U
.
This lemma is general enough to handle non-constant variance: a notable example is when
\sigma^{2}(f(x))
is linear in
f(x)
and where the resulting potential
U
then depends logarithmically on the value
f
.

Proof.

Denote
P_{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
the orthogonal projection on the eigenspace of
H^{\ast}
associated with the eigenvalue
\lambda
.
U
can thus be rewritten as

(E.3.7)

.U(x)=\sum_{\lambda\in\eig H^{\ast}}\frac{2g^{\lambda}(P_{\lambda}x)}{\lambda}\,,

so that its gradient is given by

(E.3.8)

\nabla U(x)=\sum_{\lambda\in\eig H^{\ast}}\frac{2P_{\lambda}\nabla g^{\lambda}(P_{\lambda}x)}{\lambda}\,.

In particular, we obtain that for
x
close enough to
\mathcal{K}_{i}
, using the orthogonality of the projections,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{H}}(x,\nabla U(x))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sigma^{2}(f(x))}{2}\langle\nabla U(x),H^{\ast}\nabla U(x)\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}(f(x))}{2}\sum_{\lambda\in\eig H^{\ast}}\frac{4\lVert P_{\lambda}\nabla g^{\lambda}(P_{\lambda}x)\rVert^{2}}{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}(f(x))}{2}\left\langle\nabla U(x),2\nabla(\alpha\circ f)(x)\right\rangle

(E.3.9)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\nabla U(x),\nabla f(x)\right\rangle\,.

Therefore, for
x
close enough to
\mathcal{K}_{i}
, we have that

(E.3.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(x,\nabla U(x))

\displaystyle=-\left\langle\nabla U(x),\nabla f(x)\right\rangle+\bar{\mathcal{H}}(x,\nabla U(x))\leq 0\,.

For
x
close to
\mathcal{K}_{i}
, let us compute
\langle\nabla U(x),\nabla f(x)\rangle
:

\displaystyle\langle\nabla U(x),\nabla f(x)\rangle

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2}(f(x))\langle\nabla U(x),\nabla(\alpha\circ f)(x)\rangle

(E.3.11)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2}(f(x))\sum_{\lambda\in\eig H^{\ast}}\frac{2\lVert P_{\lambda}\nabla g^{\lambda}(P_{\lambda}x\rVert}{\lambda}\,,

which is (stricly) positive in a small neighborhood of
\mathcal{K}_{i}
(excluding
\mathcal{K}_{i}
itself). Therefore,
U
is decreasing on trajectories of the flow in this neighborhood. With the same proof as in
Lemma

D.29
, we deduce that there exists
\delta>0
such that
\argmin_{x\in\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})}U(x)=\mathcal{K}_{i}
.
Moreover, take
\delta>0
small enough such that
Eq.

E.3.10
holds on
\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
,
\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})\cap\crit f=\mathcal{K}_{i}
and the trajectories of the flow started in
\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
stay converge to
\mathcal{K}_{i}
.
We now proceed as in the proof of
Lemma

D.33
.
Take
\delta^{\prime}\leq\delta/2
,
\mathcal{U}\coloneqq\mathcal{U}_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
and
\Delta\coloneqq\min\left\{U(x)-U_{i}:x\in\mathcal{X},\,d(x,\mathcal{K}_{i})=\delta^{\prime}\right\}
, which is positive by definition.
Fix
j\neq i
: we show that
B_{i,j}\geq\Delta
.
Consider some
T>0
and
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}([0,T],\mathcal{X})
such that
\gamma_{0}\in\mathcal{K}_{i}
and
\gamma_{T}\in\mathcal{K}_{j}
. By definition of
\delta
and by continuity of
\gamma
and
d(\cdot,\mathcal{K}_{i})
, there exists
t\in[0,T]
such that
d(\gamma_{t},\mathcal{K}_{i})=\delta^{\prime}
.
Therefore, we have that

(E.3.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{0,T}(\gamma)

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{S}_{0,t}(\gamma)=\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{s},\dot{\gamma}_{s})\>ds\,,

and therefore, by definition of
\mathcal{L}
as the conjugate of
\mathcal{H}
, we obtain that

(E.3.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{0,T}(\gamma)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\dot{\gamma}_{s},\nabla U(\gamma_{s})\right\rangle-\bar{\mathcal{H}}(\gamma_{s},\nabla U(\gamma_{s}))\>ds\geq\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\dot{\gamma}_{s},\nabla U(\gamma_{s})\right\rangle\,,

where we used
Eq.

E.3.10
in the last inequality.
Thus, we get

(E.3.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{0,T}(\gamma)

\displaystyle\geq U(\gamma_{t})-U(\gamma_{0})\geq\Delta\,,

with any
\delta^{\prime}\leq\delta/2
(and therefore
\delta
in the statement corresponds to
\delta/2
here).

Finally, it remains to show that, any
l\neq i
,
E_{l}\geq\Delta
.
Consider any tree rooted at
l
: it must have an edge of the form
i\to j
for some
j\in I
and therefore the sum of its weights will be at least
B_{i,j}\geq\Delta
. Hence, since it holds for any such tree, we have that
E_{l}\geq\Delta
.

We now prove the second part of the lemma.
\delta
was chosen small enough so that it is possible to find
R>\delta
such that
\mathbb{B}(0,R)\setminus\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
contains all the
\mathcal{K}_{j}
for
j\neq i
and not
\mathcal{K}_{i}
.
The assumption on the Lagrangian implies that, for any
x\in\mathbb{B}(0,R)\setminus\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
,
v\in\mathbb{B}(0,c)
,

(E.3.15)

\mathcal{L}(x,v)\leq\frac{\lVert v+\nabla f(x)\rVert^{2}}{2s^{2}}\,.

Fix
j\neq i
and take
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}^{1}([0,T],\mathcal{X})
such that
\gamma_{0}\in\mathcal{K}_{j}
,
x\coloneqq\gamma_{T}\in\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
and
\gamma
remains in
\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,R)\setminus\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
.
Without loss of generality, at the expense of replacing
\gamma
by a reparametrization, we can assume that
\dot{\gamma}_{t}
in
\mathbb{B}(0,c)
for all
t\in[0,T]
.
Then, we have that

(E.3.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{0,T}(\gamma)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{s},\dot{\gamma}_{s})\>ds\leq\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\lVert\dot{\gamma}_{s}+\nabla f(\gamma_{s})\rVert^{2}}{2s^{2}}\>ds\,,

which depends only on the value of
\nabla f
outside of
\mathcal{U}_{\delta/2}(\mathcal{K}_{i})
.
But, by the same reasoning as in
Lemma

D.31
, since the flow started at
x
converges to
\mathcal{K}_{i}
, we have that
B(\{x\},\mathcal{K}_{i})=0
. Therefore, we have that,

(E.3.17)

B_{j,i}\leq\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\lVert\dot{\gamma}_{s}+\nabla f(\gamma_{s})\rVert^{2}}{2s^{2}}\>ds\,,

and, taking the maximum of such quantities over all
j\neq i
, we obtain
C>0
such, for any
j\neq i
,

(E.3.18)

B_{j,i}\leq\frac{C}{s^{2}}\,.

To conclude on the value of
E_{i}
, we consider the tree rooted at
i
made of all the edges
(j,i)
for
j\neq i
. It has weight at most
(K-1)C/s^{2}
and therefore, we have that
E_{i}\leq(K-1)C/s^{2}
.
∎

Appendix F
Auxiliary results

F.1.
Truncated Gaussian distribution

Lemma F.1
.

Consider
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
a multivariate Gaussian distribution
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
positive definite.
For
R>0
, define the truncated Gaussian
rv
(
rv
)
X_{R}
by conditioning
X
to the ball
\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,R)
.
Define

(F.1.1)

\bar{\mathcal{H}}(p)\coloneqq\log\ex\left[e^{\langle p,X_{R}\rangle}\right]

and

(F.1.2)

\widetilde{E}(\Sigma,\widetilde{R})\coloneqq e^{-\tfrac{\widetilde{R}^{2}}{4\lVert\Sigma\rVert}}(\tr\Sigma+\lVert\Sigma\rVert)2^{d+3}

Then, for
\widetilde{R}>0
such that

(F.1.3)

\widetilde{R}\geq\sqrt{\lVert\Sigma\rVert(2d+4)\log 2}\,,

it holds that, for any
p\in\mathcal{X}
such that
\lVert\Sigma p\rVert\leq R-\widetilde{R}
,

(F.1.4a)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\bar{\mathcal{H}}(p)-\tfrac{1}{2}\langle p,\Sigma p\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\widetilde{E}(\Sigma,\widetilde{R})\lVert p\rVert^{2}

(F.1.4b)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla\bar{\mathcal{H}}(p)-\Sigma p\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{E}(\Sigma,\widetilde{R})\lVert p\rVert

(F.1.4c)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Hess\bar{\mathcal{H}}(p)-\Sigma\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{E}(\Sigma,\widetilde{R})\,.

Proof.

Define, for
S
a measurable set,

(F.1.5)

Z(S)=\int_{S}e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}{x\cdot\Sigma^{-1}x}}\>dx\,,

and, for convenience,
\mathcal{K}\coloneqq\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,R)
.
For notational convenience, in this proof, we will denote the inner product between two vectors
x
and
p
with a simple dot
x\cdot p
.
We have that

\displaystyle\ex\left[e^{X_{R}\cdot p}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\tfrac{1}{2}p\cdot\Sigma p}}{Z(\mathcal{K})}\int_{\mathcal{K}}e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}(x-\Sigma p)\cdot\Sigma^{-1}(x-\Sigma p)}\>dx

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\tfrac{1}{2}p\cdot\Sigma p}}{Z(\mathcal{K})}\int_{\mathcal{K}-\Sigma p}e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}x^{\prime}\cdot\Sigma^{-1}x^{\prime}}\>dx^{\prime}

(F.1.6)

\displaystyle=e^{\tfrac{1}{2}p\cdot\Sigma p}\frac{Z(\mathcal{K}-\Sigma p)}{Z(\mathcal{K})}\,,

where we performed the change of variable
x^{\prime}\leftarrow x-\Sigma p
.

Define

(F.1.7)

f(p)\coloneqq Z(\mathcal{K}-\Sigma p)=\int_{\mathcal{K}}e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}(x-\Sigma p)\cdot\Sigma^{-1}(x-\Sigma p)}\>dx\,.

so that

(F.1.8)

\bar{\mathcal{H}}(p)=\log\ex\left[e^{X_{R}\cdot p}\right]={\frac{1}{2}}p\cdot\Sigma p+\log\frac{f(p)}{f(0)}\,.

Therefore it suffices to bound
\log\frac{f(p)}{f(0)}
and its derivatives.

Differentiating yields

(F.1.9a)

\displaystyle\nabla f(p)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{K}}(x-\Sigma p)e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}(x-\Sigma p)\cdot\Sigma^{-1}(x-\Sigma p)}\>dx

(F.1.9b)

\displaystyle\Hess f(p)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{K}}(x-\Sigma p)(x-\Sigma p)^{\top}e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}(x-\Sigma p)\cdot\Sigma^{-1}(x-\Sigma p)}\>dx-\Sigma f(p)\,.

Note that, by symmetry of
\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,R)
,
\nabla f(0)=0
.

We first bound
\Hess f(p)
.
Performing the change of variable
x^{\prime}\leftarrow x-\Sigma p
again gives us

\displaystyle\Hess f(p)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{K}-\Sigma p}x^{\prime}x^{\prime\top}e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}x^{\prime}\cdot\Sigma^{-1}x^{\prime}}\>dx^{\prime}-\Sigma f(p)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{K}-\Sigma p}(x^{\prime}x^{\prime\top}-\Sigma)e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}x^{\prime}\cdot\Sigma^{-1}x^{\prime}}\>dx^{\prime}

(F.1.10)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathcal{X}\setminus(\mathcal{K}-\Sigma p)}(x^{\prime}x^{\prime\top}-\Sigma)e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}x^{\prime}\cdot\Sigma^{-1}x^{\prime}}\>dx^{\prime}\,,

where we used that
\int_{\mathcal{X}}(x^{\prime}x^{\prime\top}-\Sigma)e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}x^{\prime}\cdot\Sigma^{-1}x^{\prime}}\>dx^{\prime}=0
.

By definition of
\widetilde{R}
,
\mathcal{K}-\Sigma p
contains
\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,\widetilde{R})
.
We now bound the operator norm of
\Hess f(p)
:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Hess f(p)\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{X}\setminus(\mathcal{K}-\Sigma p)}(\lVert x^{\prime}\rVert^{2}+\lVert\Sigma\rVert)e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}x^{\prime}\cdot\Sigma^{-1}x^{\prime}}\>dx^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{X}\setminus\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,\widetilde{R})}(\lVert x^{\prime}\rVert^{2}+\lVert\Sigma\rVert)e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}x^{\prime}\cdot\Sigma^{-1}x^{\prime}}\>dx^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\tfrac{\widetilde{R}^{2}}{4\lVert\Sigma\rVert}}\int_{\mathcal{X}}(\lVert x^{\prime}\rVert^{2}+\lVert\Sigma\rVert)e^{-\tfrac{1}{4}x^{\prime}\cdot\Sigma^{-1}x^{\prime}}\>dx^{\prime}

(F.1.11)

\displaystyle=e^{-\tfrac{\widetilde{R}^{2}}{4\lVert\Sigma\rVert}}(\tr\Sigma+\lVert\Sigma\rVert)(4\pi)^{d/2}\det(\Sigma)^{1/2}\,,

We now bound
\nabla f(p)
. The change of variable
x^{\prime}\leftarrow x-\Sigma p
yields

(F.1.12)

\displaystyle\nabla f(p)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{K}-\Sigma p}x^{\prime}e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}x^{\prime}\cdot\Sigma^{-1}x^{\prime}}\>dx^{\prime}=\int_{\mathcal{X}\setminus(\mathcal{K}-\Sigma p)}x^{\prime}e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}x^{\prime}\cdot\Sigma^{-1}x^{\prime}}\>dx^{\prime}\,,

where we used that
\int_{\mathcal{X}}x^{\prime}e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}x^{\prime}\cdot\Sigma^{-1}x^{\prime}}\>dx^{\prime}=0
.

Therefore, similar computations as above yield that

\displaystyle\lVert\nabla f(p)\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{X}\setminus(\mathcal{K}-\Sigma p)}\lVert x^{\prime}\rVert e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}x^{\prime}\cdot\Sigma^{-1}x^{\prime}}\>dx^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{X}\setminus\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,\widetilde{R})}\lVert x^{\prime}\rVert e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}x^{\prime}\cdot\Sigma^{-1}x^{\prime}}\>dx^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\tfrac{\widetilde{R}^{2}}{4\lVert\Sigma\rVert}}\int_{\mathcal{X}}\lVert x^{\prime}\rVert e^{-\tfrac{1}{4}x^{\prime}\cdot\Sigma^{-1}x^{\prime}}\>dx^{\prime}

(F.1.13)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\tfrac{\widetilde{R}^{2}}{4\lVert\Sigma\rVert}}\sqrt{\tr\Sigma}(4\pi)^{d/2}\det(\Sigma)^{1/2}\,.

Let us now lower-bound
f(p)
.
In the same fashion as above, we have that

\displaystyle f(p)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{K}-\Sigma p}e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}x^{\prime}\cdot\Sigma^{-1}x^{\prime}}\>dx^{\prime}

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,\widetilde{R})}e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}x^{\prime}\cdot\Sigma^{-1}x^{\prime}}\>dx^{\prime}

(F.1.14)

\displaystyle\geq(2\pi)^{d/2}\det(\Sigma)^{1/2}\left(1-2^{d/2}e^{-\tfrac{\widetilde{R}^{2}}{4\lVert\Sigma\rVert}}\right)\,,

so that, if
\widetilde{R}\geq\sqrt{\lVert\Sigma\rVert(2d+4)\log 2}
,
it holds that

(F.1.15)

f(p)\geq\tfrac{1}{2}(2\pi)^{d/2}\det(\Sigma)^{1/2}

The Hessian of
\log f/f(0)
is then given by

(F.1.16)

\Hess\log\frac{f(p)}{f(0)}=\frac{\Hess f(p)}{f(p)}-\frac{\nabla f(p)\nabla f(p)^{\top}}{f(p)^{2}}\,.

Eqs.

F.1.11
,
F.1.13
and
F.1.15
combined yield that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Hess\log\frac{f(p)}{f(0)}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\tfrac{\widetilde{R}^{2}}{4\lVert\Sigma\rVert}}(\tr\Sigma+\lVert\Sigma\rVert)2^{d/2+1}+\left(e^{-\tfrac{\widetilde{R}^{2}}{4\lVert\Sigma\rVert}}\sqrt{\tr\Sigma}2^{d/2+1}\right)^{2}

(F.1.17)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\tfrac{\widetilde{R}^{2}}{4\lVert\Sigma\rVert}}(\tr\Sigma+\lVert\Sigma\rVert)2^{d+3}=\widetilde{E}(\Sigma,\widetilde{R})\,.

Taylor-Lagrange inequality now yields the full result since
\log f(0)/f(0)=0
and
\nabla\log f(0)=0
.
∎

Lemma F.2
.

Consider
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I)
a multivariate Gaussian distribution with
\sigma^{2}>0
. For
R>0
, define the truncated Gaussian
rv

X_{R}
by conditioning
X
to the ball
\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,R)
.
Define

(F.1.18a)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{H}}(p)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\log\ex\left[e^{\langle p,X_{R}\rangle}\right]

(F.1.18b)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{L}}(p)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{*}(p)\,,

and

(F.1.19)

E(\sigma^{2},R)\coloneqq e^{-\tfrac{R^{2}}{16\sigma^{2}}}2^{d+3}(d+1)

and assume that
R>0
satisfies

(F.1.20)

R\geq 4\sigma\sqrt{(d+3)\log 2+\log(d+1)}\,.

Then, for any
p\in\mathcal{X}
such that
\lVert p\rVert\leq\frac{R}{2\sigma^{2}}
,
v\in\mathcal{X}
such that
\lVert v\rVert\leq\frac{R}{4}
, it holds that

(F.1.21a)

\displaystyle\left(1-E(\sigma^{2},R)\right)\frac{\sigma^{2}\lVert p\rVert^{2}}{2}\leq

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{H}}(p)\leq\left(1+E(\sigma^{2},R)\right)\frac{\sigma^{2}\lVert p\rVert^{2}}{2}

(F.1.21b)

\displaystyle\left(1-2E(\sigma^{2},R)\right)\frac{\lVert v\rVert^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\leq

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{L}}(v)\leq\left(1+2E(\sigma^{2},R)\right)\frac{\lVert v\rVert^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\,.

Proof.

First, let us show that, for any
r>0
,
\nabla\bar{\mathcal{H}}\left(\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,r)\right)
is a closed ball centered at 0.
Since the distribution of
X_{R}
is invariant by rotation, this set can be rewritten as

(F.1.22)

\nabla\bar{\mathcal{H}}\left(\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,r)\right)=\left\{v\in\mathcal{X}:\lVert v\rVert\in\left\{\left\lVert\nabla\bar{\mathcal{H}}(p)\right\rVert:p\in\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,r)\right\}\right\}\,.

But, by connectedness of
\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,r)
and continuity of
\nabla\bar{\mathcal{H}}
,
\left\{\left\lVert\nabla\bar{\mathcal{H}}(p)\right\rVert:p\in\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,r)\right\}
is an interval. Since
\nabla\bar{\mathcal{H}}(0)=0
, it is either
\left[0,\sup_{\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,r)}\left\lVert\nabla\bar{\mathcal{H}}\right\rVert\right]
or
\left[0,\sup_{\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,r)}\left\lVert\nabla\bar{\mathcal{H}}\right\rVert\right)
.
\nabla\bar{\mathcal{H}}\left(\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,r)\right)
being compact and therefore closed, it must be the latter.
Hence, we have shown that

(F.1.23)

\nabla\bar{\mathcal{H}}\left(\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,r)\right)=\overline{\mathbb{B}}\left(0,\sup_{\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,r)}\left\lVert\nabla\bar{\mathcal{H}}\right\rVert\right)\,.

We apply
Lemma

F.1
with
\widetilde{R}\leftarrow{\frac{R}{2}}
. Note that our choice of
R
implies that
\widetilde{R}
satisfies the condition of
Lemma

F.1
and, moreover, that

E(\sigma^{2},R)\leq{\frac{1}{2}}
.
Lemma

F.1
directly implies the bound on
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
.

Consider
p\in\mathcal{X}
such that
\lVert p\rVert\leq\frac{R}{2\sigma^{2}}
.
Then, by
Lemma

F.1
, we have that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla\bar{\mathcal{H}}\left(p\right)\right\rVert

\displaystyle\geq\sigma^{2}\lVert p\rVert-e^{-\tfrac{R^{2}}{16\sigma^{2}}}2^{d+3}\left(\tr\left(\sigma^{2}I\right)+\left\lVert\sigma^{2}I\right\rVert\right)\lVert p\rVert

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2}\lVert p\rVert(1-E(\sigma^{2},R))

(F.1.24)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\sigma^{2}\lVert p\rVert}{2}\,,

where we used that
E(\sigma^{2},R)\leq{\frac{1}{2}}
.
Therefore, we obtain that
\sup\left\{\left\lVert\nabla\bar{\mathcal{H}}(p)\right\rVert:p\in\overline{\mathbb{B}}\left(0,\frac{R}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)\right\}\geq\frac{R}{4}
so that
\nabla\bar{\mathcal{H}}\left(\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,\frac{R}{2\sigma^{2}})\right)
contains
\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,\frac{R}{4})
.

Take
v\in\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,\frac{R}{4})
which therefore belongs to
\nabla\bar{\mathcal{H}}\left(\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,\frac{R}{2\sigma^{2}})\right)
.
Therefore,
\bar{\mathcal{L}}(v)
can be rewritten as

(F.1.25)

\bar{\mathcal{L}}(v)=\sup_{p\in\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,\frac{R}{2\sigma^{2}})}\langle v,p\rangle-\bar{\mathcal{H}}(p)\,.

Using
Lemma

F.1
again, we obtain that

(F.1.26)

\displaystyle\sup_{p\in\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,\frac{R}{2\sigma^{2}})}\langle v,p\rangle-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}(1+E(\sigma^{2},R))\lVert p\rVert^{2}\leq\bar{\mathcal{L}}(v)\leq\sup_{p\in\overline{\mathbb{B}}(0,\frac{R}{2\sigma^{2}})}\langle v,p\rangle-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}(1-E(\sigma^{2},R))\lVert p\rVert^{2}\,,

so that, since
E(\sigma^{2},R)\leq{\frac{1}{2}}
, we obtain that

(F.1.27)

\frac{\lVert v\rVert^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}(1+E(\sigma^{2},R))}\leq\bar{\mathcal{L}}(v)\leq\frac{\lVert v\rVert^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}(1-E(\sigma^{2},R))}\,.

Since, for
x\in[0,1/2]
, both
\frac{1}{1+x}\geq 1-2x
and
\frac{1}{1-x}\leq 1+2x
hold,
we get

(F.1.28)

\frac{\lVert v\rVert^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\left(1-2E(\sigma^{2},R)\right)\leq\bar{\mathcal{L}}(v)\leq\frac{\lVert v\rVert^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\left(1+2E(\sigma^{2},R)\right)

which concludes the proof.
∎

We will require the following technical lemma.

Lemma F.3
.

Consider
v,w\in\mathcal{X}
such that
0<\lVert w\rVert\leq\tfrac{\mu R}{2}
for some
R,\mu>0
.
Define,

(F.1.29)

f(u)=\sup_{p\in\mathcal{X}:\lVert p\rVert\leq R}\langle p,u\rangle-{\frac{\mu}{2}}\lVert p\rVert^{2}\,,

then, with
\lambda=\frac{\lVert v\rVert}{\lVert w\rVert}
,

(F.1.30)

\lambda f\left(\frac{v}{\lambda}+w\right)\leq f(v+w)\,.

Proof.

Define
p\coloneqq\frac{1}{\mu}\left(\frac{v}{\lambda}+w\right)
which has norm at most
R
since
\lVert w\rVert\leq\frac{R}{2\mu}
.
Then, we have that

\displaystyle\lambda f\left(\frac{v}{\lambda}+w\right)-f(v+w)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda\left(\left\langle p,\frac{v}{\lambda}+w\right\rangle-{\frac{\mu}{2}}\lVert p\rVert^{2}\right)-\left(\langle p,v+w\rangle-{\frac{\mu}{2}}\lVert p\rVert^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=(\lambda-1)\left(\langle p,w\rangle-{\frac{\mu}{2}}\lVert p\rVert^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=(\lambda-1)\times\frac{1}{\mu}\left(\frac{\langle v,w\rangle}{\lambda}+\lVert w\rVert^{2}-{\frac{1}{2}}\left(2\lVert w\rVert^{2}+2\frac{\langle v,w\rangle}{\lambda}\right)\right)

(F.1.31)

\displaystyle=0\,,

and our proof is complete.
∎
